One reason why a better model is unavailable follows (`GenerationView.infoReason(status:tier:overrides:)`): under default styling, the on-device model's, such as "Apple's on-device model isn't ready yet. It may still be downloading.", or "This iPhone doesn't support Apple Intelligence." when the device supports neither of Apple's models; otherwise Private Cloud Compute's, such as "This iPhone doesn't support Private Cloud Compute." Builds without Private Cloud Compute never give its reason, and neither does the info button while your model designs because **Use this model instead of Private Cloud Compute** is on. While Apple Intelligence is off, the info button gives no reason, because the footer says how to turn it on.

In **Design model** on the iPhone and **Settings > Design Model** on the Mac, Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google Gemini each have a **Model name** picker of current models from the [design model list](#design-model-list), with **Custom…** for any other model ID, which shows a **Model ID** field. A star marks each provider's recommended model, the fast, low-cost one that a new choice starts with: **★ Claude Haiku 5.5** (`claude-haiku-5-5`), **★ GPT-6 Luna** (`gpt-6-luna`), or **★ Gemini 3.8 Flash** (`gemini-3.8-flash`). The list also offers a stronger model for each provider, such as Claude Sonnet 5.5 or Gemini 4 Argon, whose label says how its cost compares, and Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite. A saved model ID that the list names selects it; any other shows as **Custom…** with its ID, so settings saved before the picker keep their model. The OpenAI-compatible endpoint has only the **Model name** field, and its **Base URL** needs `http://` or `https://` and a host: until it has them, the Spines tab says "Enter your endpoint's base URL, starting with http:// or https://." **Thinking** appears when the list gives the chosen model thinking levels, with the provider's own level names: Gemini's `thinkingLevel`, Anthropic's effort, or OpenAI's `reasoning_effort`. It starts at the list's default for the model, its cheapest sensible level, such as `low`, and each provider keeps its own level, so switching providers and back restores it. A model ID under **Custom…** shows no **Thinking** and its requests set no level, so the provider's default applies, because a model can think yet reject a level, as Gemini 2.5 models reject `thinkingLevel`.

In builds with Private Cloud Compute, choosing a provider also shows **Use this model instead of Private Cloud Compute**, in a section of its own under the provider's settings. It is off by default, saves on this device when you change it, and doesn't sync. Its footer says that, while it is on, your model can also redesign Private Cloud Compute's spines (see [Designs from several devices](#designs-from-several-devices)). While it is off, your model designs only when Private Cloud Compute isn't available, not when its daily limit is reached, because a run then waits for the reset. The Spines tab's **Model** row follows the change at once. `NO_PCC` builds never show the switch and ignore a saved choice. With **Apple (default)** chosen, the settings' footer says that Private Cloud Compute designs spines and that Apple's on-device model designs when it isn't available, or in `NO_PCC` builds that the on-device model designs spines on this device. A preset provider's footer says that it bills your API account and receives each book's cover, title, and author, and that your key stays on this device, such as "Google Gemini bills your API account and receives each book's cover, title, and author. Your key stays on this iPhone." A custom endpoint's footer says that your endpoint receives each book's cover, title, and author, and that your key, if it needs one, stays on this device; it claims no bill. How many requests a design takes shows only in the Spines tab's info button.

When the screen opens, when you save a key, and when you choose a preset provider's model, the app asks the provider's get-model endpoint (Gemini's `GET v1beta/models/{id}`, or Anthropic's or OpenAI's `GET v1/models/{id}`) whether the key can use the model: one request, away from the main thread, which reads the key from the Keychain and never shows or logs it. An HTTP 403 or 404 shows "This key can't use `model-id`. Choose another model." under the picker, and a rejected key (HTTP 401 or Gemini's `API_KEY_INVALID`) shows "*Provider* didn't accept your API key. Remove it and add a new one." Saving never waits for the check, the check never retries, and a network failure, a timeout, or any other answer shows nothing.

The API key is saved in this device's Keychain when you press Return, when its field loses focus, or when you leave the screen or close Settings, with a "Key saved" toast on the iPhone. When you choose another **Model**, a key you typed saves to the provider you typed it for, without a toast, and without a message if the Keychain refuses it. Before a provider's first key, or the OpenAI-compatible endpoint's first base URL, saves on a device, an alert says "Bookworms will send each book's cover, title, and author to *Provider* to design spines.", with "your endpoint" for the OpenAI-compatible endpoint, and offers **Allow** and **Cancel**. **Cancel** saves nothing and leaves a key's field empty or restores the saved base URL. **Allow** saves the key or base URL and remembers on this device that you allowed that provider (`SpineDesigner.hasConsent(of:)`), so its later keys and base URLs save without asking. Every request to a provider, and the Spines tab's check that the provider is set up, read that answer first (`SpineDesigner.requestKey(loadKey:)`). A provider you haven't allowed on this device counts as not set up, even when the Keychain holds its key, which can happen after you delete the app and install it again: deleting clears the answer and can leave the key. The Spines tab then says "Add your *Provider* API key.", the screen shows the key field instead of **Saved**, no request goes to the provider, and the same alert asks when the screen opens on that provider or you choose it. **Allow** makes the saved key count. **Cancel** removes that key from the Keychain, so the alert asks about a leftover key once. A key or base URL for a provider you haven't allowed is dropped when it would save without the alert: when you leave the screen, close Settings, or choose another **Model** (`SpineDesignerSettings.saveChoice(for:closing:)`). Apple's models never ask. The key is never synced, and shows only as **Saved** with **Remove key** afterward. At launch, the app checks away from the main thread which providers have a saved key that their requests can carry (`SpineDesigner.Provider.savedKey()`), without reading the keys, and keeps only those flags in memory, so the screen shows **Saved** in its first frame. It checks the chosen provider again when the screen opens and when you choose a provider, and a check never undoes a save or removal that finished while it ran. A test copy's preview launch (`TestCopy`, `-BookwormsPreviewFolder`) keeps keys in its own Keychain service, `gay.ian.Bookworms.ai-provider.scratch`, so it never reads yours. Every iPhone build counts as a test copy, so only the installed Mac app's preview launches use your key. A key the Keychain refuses shows "Couldn't save the key. Try again." under the field. A typed model ID, model name, or base URL saves the same way, so typing reads no Keychain item; a picker choice saves at once. The provider receives each book's whole cover, its title and author, and the lines Vision read on the cover, with fixed questions about the title lettering (`BookAnalyzer.readCover`), or, when it rejects the whole cover, only the title lettering cut from it; a preset provider bills your API account. No model receives a book's description. The providers plug into Apple's FoundationModels framework as `LanguageModel` implementations (`Bookworms/Services/SpineDesigner.swift`), so `BookAnalyzer` asks them the same questions, with the same image and JSON schemas, as Private Cloud Compute. A provider's model declares FoundationModels' reasoning capability only with a thinking level (`ProviderModel`), and then every request carries your level in the provider's terms: `generationConfig.thinkingConfig.thinkingLevel` for Gemini, `output_config.effort` for Anthropic, or `reasoning_effort` for OpenAI. Without a level, requests set none and the provider's default applies. A session refuses a request with a reasoning level for a model without the capability before sending it, so the cover-lettering request asks for one only when the model reasons (`BookAnalyzer.readingOptions(for:)`). Each request goes to Anthropic's Messages API with structured outputs, OpenAI's or the custom endpoint's chat completions with a JSON schema response format, or Gemini's `generateContent` with a response JSON schema; the schema is also stated in the system prompt for endpoints that ignore it. When the provider rejects the key or the model name, or reports an account without credit (HTTP 401, 402, 403, or 404, OpenAI's `insufficient_quota`, or Gemini's `API_KEY_INVALID`), the Spines tab says what is wrong (`ProviderError.errorDescription`): "*Provider* didn't accept your API key." for a 401 or `API_KEY_INVALID`, "Your *provider* account is out of credit." for a 402 or `insufficient_quota`, or "*Provider* can't use this model with your key." for a 403 or 404. For a custom endpoint, a 401 or 403 shows "Your endpoint didn't accept your API key.", or "Your endpoint refused the request without an API key." when no key is saved, a 404 "Your endpoint didn't find this model. Check the model name and base URL.", and any other error "Your endpoint:" with its message. The run's message adds that Bookworms uses the next tier until you change your model settings or tap **Generate now** (click on the Mac), and the run designs that book and the rest of its books with the next tier available, usually the on-device model or local defaults. When that tier can't take a request either, such as Private Cloud Compute at its daily limit, the run stops with the rejection. A rejection that arrives after you replaced the key fails only that book. Any other refusal fails only that book, which waits 24 hours, and the run goes on with the backup: an HTTP status that isn't a rate limit or a server error, such as a 400 for one cover's title crop or a 422, a reply without an answer ("The response had no answer."), or an answer that isn't the JSON the request asked for ("The design model's answer wasn't the JSON Bookworms asked for."). When the backup refuses three books in a row with the same message, as for an Anthropic account without credit, a model that doesn't take images, an endpoint that doesn't take the JSON schema format or ignores it, or a wrong base URL that answers with a web page, those books still wait 24 hours, and the message counts as a lasting rejection for the rest of the run. Anthropic answers an account without credit with an HTTP 400 whose message mentions the credit balance, which shows "Your Anthropic account is out of credit."; any other 400 shows the provider's name, or "Your endpoint", and its own message. With several books in flight, "in a row" follows the order in which the provider's answers arrive, and the first lasting rejection moves the run to the next tier once: the other books whose requests the backup rejects or refuses meanwhile are checked again at that tier, one at a time, in their places in the queue. The app remembers a lasting rejection on this device, so later runs start at the next tier and the Spines tab shows the message beside **Choose model**; the record names the settings and whether a key exists, never the key. The rejection lasts until you change the provider, model, thinking level, base URL, or key, which also clears the last run's message that the next tier designs, or start **Generate now** or **Redesign spine**, which try the backup again in case you fixed the cause elsewhere, such as by adding credit or loading the model on a local server. A rate limit or an endpoint whose host the app can't find stops the run, and the next attempt waits for the provider's `Retry-After` time or 15 minutes. App Transport Security allows an `http://` base URL only for `localhost`, an IP address, a `.local` name, or a host name without a dot. It blocks `http://` to any other host name, which counts as a lasting rejection: "Your endpoint's base URL needs https://. Only local servers can use http://." The first request to an endpoint on another device on your network, with either scheme, makes iOS or macOS ask once for Local Network access, with the text "Bookworms connects to the model endpoint you set up on your network to design spines." (`NSLocalNetworkUsageDescription` in `project.yml`); a server on the same Mac, such as `localhost`, never asks. Only the endpoint's base URL can name a local address: covers and profile photos download over HTTPS from public host names only (`CoverURLPolicy`), and the app's other requests go to fixed hosts, which are Hardcover, Google Fonts, the preset providers, and Apple's services. A request that times out, loses its connection, can't connect, such as to a local server that is off, or gets a bad reply or a server error (HTTP 500 or above) fails only its book: the book tries again after about 2 and then 4 seconds, then Apple's Private Cloud Compute or on-device model designs it when one is available, one such book at a time, and otherwise the book is skipped and tried again a day later, while the run goes on with the other books. When Apple's model hits a problem that stops any run, such as a Google Fonts outage, its daily limit, or a lost network, the run stops as it would at that tier. The third book in a row that can't reach your model this way, in the order the answers arrive, stops the run instead, even when Apple's models designed the two before it: that book stays queued, and the next attempt waits 15 minutes. A book that your model designs or answers in between starts the count again. A later run with your model designs again a book that Apple's model designed in its place, whether the on-device model or Private Cloud Compute, unless you changed its colors or font since; this device's ledger records the stand-in (`WorkLedger.Entry.standInFor`) and its generation time, so another design saved since, such as another device's, stays, and a failed check keeps the record. When your model still can't answer, the book keeps the stand-in and is tried again a day later, instead of getting another stand-in, unless a better Apple model is available now: Private Cloud Compute then replaces the on-device model's stand-in, and the new design is still a stand-in. A newly saved stand-in decides no series look, unless it answers **Redesign spine** with **Changes apply to** set to **Whole series**: then it remakes the look and stays. A look made from a stand-in, by the app's catch-up or by a check that keeps the stand-in and upgrades its group's look, gives way with it (see [Series designs](#series-designs)). A check that keeps a stand-in, such as a run at Private Cloud Compute while your model is off, keeps it recorded, so your model's next run still designs the book again. So does a redo whose design comes from a tier below the stand-in's, such as the on-device model's after a lasting rejection moved the redo of a Private Cloud Compute stand-in to it: the stand-in stays. A stand-in whose colors or font you changed stays, and its check drops the record, so your model stops checking it on every run. A Private Cloud Compute design that no run made in your model's place, such as one from a run at Private Cloud Compute, is no stand-in and stays. When the Keychain refuses to read a saved key, as before the first unlock after a restart, the **Spines** tab says it couldn't read the key, and runs stop without sending a request until a later run can read it, instead of designing with another model; the next background run tries 3 hours later. A key that the Keychain fails to read for any other reason counts as no key, and no request goes to Anthropic, OpenAI, or Google without a key.

Local defaults pick families from the book's first Hardcover genre that the table below recognizes, matching lowercased substrings in this order, as the Apple TV's own genre spines do (`BookAnalyzer.genreFamilies(_:)`, after the tvOS app's `BookWallLocalSpine.fontName(for:)`). The title measurements then rank those families, as for a model's answers. With no recognized genre, they rank the serif and sans families except rounded ones. Without a title line that ranks the families, such as none measured or one in a script the font atlas lacks, the genre's families, or without a recognized genre the humanist sans faces, come in the book's own reproducible order at weight 700. Only the young-adult and middle-grade row gets rounded faces. Blocked families and `SpineFamily.neverGenerated` are never offered.

| Genre contains | Families |
| --- | --- |
| litrpg, fantasy, magic | Classical capitals and book serifs |
| science fiction, sci-fi, space opera, cyberpunk | Geometric sans |
| mystery, thriller, crime | Condensed sans |
| horror, gothic | Condensed sans |
| romance, love story | Humanist sans |
| history, historical | Book serifs |
| literary, classics | Humanist sans |
| biography, memoir | Humanist sans |
| young adult, middle grade | Rounded |
| graphic novel, comics | Humanist sans |
| poetry, verse | Humanist sans |
| humor, humour, satire | Monospace and Special Elite |

The Apple TV has no model of its own: an iPhone or Mac designs the books its worklist lists, each at its own best tier.

Each design records its tier in `provenance` (see [iCloud contract](#icloud-contract)), and the app records the tier of each book's last check on this device, or of the design that check kept when it comes from a better tier. A run at a better tier rechecks at once, instead of after seven days, the books whose last check ran with Apple's on-device model or default styling (see [Spine generation](#spine-generation)). When such a recheck finds a design from the on-device model or default styling, it designs the book again, unless you chose its colors or font, and its save replaces that design. A design from Private Cloud Compute or a model with an API key stays whichever model runs, even after the book's metadata or cover image changes: a run with Private Cloud Compute keeps your model's designs, and a run with your model, whether Private Cloud Compute is unavailable or you chose your model instead, keeps Private Cloud Compute's. Any other design from the run's tier or a better one stays too, except one that awaits its font (see item 4), whichever generator version made it, so a run with Apple's on-device model keeps the retired Mac companion's. Only a cover you chose replaces a design whatever its tier; the redesign button replaces a design from the run's tier or a weaker one, and with your model overriding Private Cloud Compute, your model's redesign also replaces Private Cloud Compute's (see [Designs from several devices](#designs-from-several-devices)). Series looks from the on-device model or default styling move up the same way (see [Series designs](#series-designs)).

#### Design model list

`Bookworms/Resources/DesignModels.json` lists the models that the **Model name** picker offers, and is the only place that names them: a new choice of a provider starts with its recommended model (`SpineDesigner.Provider.defaultModel`). The app reads the bundled file when it first needs the list, so an edit ships with the next build. A file that is missing or doesn't decode lists no models, and each picker offers only **Custom…**. The file has this format:

```json
{
  "checked": "2026-10-21",
  "models": [
    {
      "provider": "google",
      "id": "gemini-3.8-flash",
      "label": "Gemini 3.8 Flash — fast, low cost",
      "tier": "low-cost",
      "recommended": true,
      "thinking": ["low", "medium", "high"],
      "thinkingDefault": "low"
    }
  ]
}
```

| Field | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `checked` | The day someone last checked the list against the providers' model pages, as `yyyy-MM-dd`. |
| `provider` | `anthropic`, `openAI`, or `google`, the raw values of `SpineDesigner.Provider`. The OpenAI-compatible endpoint has no list. |
| `id` | The API model ID that requests send. |
| `label` | The picker's text: the model's name, an em dash, and a short note, such as "fast, low cost" or, for a stronger model, how its cost compares. The app adds "★ " to the recommended model, so the label has no star. |
| `tier` | `low-cost` for the fast, low-cost tier, such as Gemini Flash and Flash-Lite, Claude Haiku, or GPT-6 Luna; `stronger` for a pricier model. |
| `recommended` | `true` on exactly one `low-cost` model per provider: the cheapest model that designs spines well, never the newest or priciest one by default. Omit it on the others. |
| `thinking` | The model's thinking levels in the provider's API terms, cheapest first, as the **Thinking** picker lists them. Only a model with this field sends a level. Omit it for a model without thinking levels. |
| `thinkingDefault` | The level a new choice starts with, one of `thinking`: the cheapest sensible level. Omit it with `thinking`. |

List only models that take image input and structured outputs, because each request sends the cover, or the crop of its title line, and asks for an answer that matches a JSON schema. Keep each provider to its fast, low-cost models and at most one stronger option, in the order the picker shows them. A model removed from the list keeps working for readers who chose it: it shows as **Custom…** with its ID. Check the list against Google's [Gemini models](https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/models), [pricing](https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/pricing), and [thinking](https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/thinking) pages, Anthropic's [models overview](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/overview) and [effort](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/effort) pages, and OpenAI's [models](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/models) pages, then update `checked`. `DesignModelsTests` checks the format: one `low-cost` recommendation per provider, unique IDs, labels without a star, and a default among each model's levels.

### Designs from several devices

Each device designs with its own model. API keys stay in the Keychain of the device you add them to, and model choices don't sync, so a Mac with your Gemini key designs with Gemini while an iPhone without it designs with Apple's on-device model. The designs sync through iCloud, so every device draws the same spines, and each design names the model that made it in its `provenance` model token (see [iCloud contract](#icloud-contract)).

- The Spines tab's **Saved designs** section counts the designs the library's books draw by the model that made them, best tier first, then the most designs, in as many columns as the window's width fits, such as **Google Gemini (gemini-3.8-flash)**, **Apple on-device model**, and **Default styling**. The list includes other devices' designs, so an iPhone can list a provider whose key it lacks, and a `NO_PCC` build can list Private Cloud Compute. It reads the design's token alone, never this device's settings, so an iPhone without the Gemini key still credits Gemini's designs to Gemini. A design without a token reads as its generator's tier, such as **Private Cloud Compute** for this app's generator, even in builds without Private Cloud Compute.
- A device replaces only designs from Apple's on-device model or default styling, and only with one from a better tier: Private Cloud Compute or your model with an API key, then Apple's on-device model, then default styling. All backup models share one tier. Designs from Private Cloud Compute and from any model with an API key stay whichever device checks them. So the Mac with the key upgrades the on-device and default designs that other devices made, while an iPhone without the key, or one where Private Cloud Compute designs, keeps Gemini's designs and designs only books without a design or with an on-device or default design. When two devices design one book at once, the save from the better tier stays, whichever finishes first, unless the first save comes from Private Cloud Compute or a model with an API key, which then stays.
- Only a cover you choose replaces a better tier's design. While **Use this model instead of Private Cloud Compute** is on, your model ranks with Private Cloud Compute for a redesign, so **Redesign spine** replaces Private Cloud Compute's design on that device. On a device whose model ranks below the design's, the **Spine design** card shows a note in place of the redesign button that says where to go to redesign the spine on this device (`SpineWorker.redesignAdvice(for:deviceTier:)`). Because all backup models share one tier, a spine from any provider's model or from Apple's on-device model gets "To redesign this spine here, open “Choose model” in the Spines tab and set up your own model." A Private Cloud Compute spine gets "To redesign this spine here, open “Choose model” in the Spines tab and turn on “Use this model instead of Private Cloud Compute”." where your model designs, "To redesign this spine here, open “Choose model” in the Spines tab and set up your own model, then turn on “Use this model instead of Private Cloud Compute”." elsewhere, and in `NO_PCC` builds, which ignore the switch, "To redesign this spine, use a Bookworms build with Private Cloud Compute." A redesign that starts before this device's model status arrives leaves such a design in place, and the run's message, which shows in the Spines tab, names the model before the same advice, such as "Google Gemini (gemini-3.8-flash) made this spine, so it stays. To redesign this spine here, open “Choose model” in the Spines tab and set up your own model."

### On the Mac

The Mac app is a native macOS build of the same target and source, so it reads the same library and saves designs the Apple TV picks up when its **iCloud storage** is on. `Bookworms/Support/AppKitBridge.swift` names AppKit's image, color, and font types after UIKit's and adds the UIKit members the shared drawing code uses. Its differences from the iPhone:

- The sections (**Share**, **Library**, **Desk**, and **Spines**) are in a sidebar, which marks the selected section in your accent color, as other Mac apps do. The sidebar button beside the window's close, minimize, and zoom buttons, or **View > Show Sidebar** (Control-Command-S), shows and hides it. Above the sections, the sidebar shows your Hardcover account (see [Where the account shows](#where-the-account-shows)). The sidebar stays at least 160 points wide, so that button keeps its place there rather than moving into the toolbar's overflow menu (`SidebarMinimumWidth`). While the sidebar is hidden and its account row carries a mark, such as after Hardcover rejected the login, the sidebar button carries a "!" badge, so you know to open the sidebar (`SidebarButtonBadge`). Each launch opens on the section you last chose, or on **Share** at first launch or when a book window asked for **Share**. **Share** in a book's window opens the main window on **Share** either way.
- Most toolbar buttons show only their symbols. Rest the pointer on one, and a tooltip says what it does, such as "Back to the shelf" for **Back** or "Switch between Stack and Shelf" for **View as**. The time buttons in the middle of the Library and Desk toolbars, whose titles name the time, say "Choose which books show". VoiceOver reads the same button names, such as **View options**, on the Mac and the iPhone.
- The menu bar holds every Library command (`LibraryCommands`), which act on the window in front:
  - **File > Open in New Window** (Option-Command-O) opens the centered book's details in a window of their own (see [Library](#library)).
  - **View** offers **as Stack** and **as Shelf** (Control-Command-1 and Control-Command-2), **3D Books**, and the **Time** and **Sort By** menus while Library shows its books.
  - **Go > Back** (Command-[) closes a book's details, from Library or Desk, and Command-1 through Command-4 switch sections.
  - **Library** offers **Find Book…** (Command-F); for the centered book, **Open** (Command-O), **Make Share Image**, **Change Cover…**, **Redesign Spine**, and **Copy Title**; and **Sync Library** (Command-R), **Generate Spines** or **Stop Generating** (Shift-Command-G), and **Hardcover Account…**.
  - The app has no help book, so the **Help** menu holds the system's search and **Privacy Policy**, which opens https://ian.gay/bookworms-privacy-policy/ in the default browser. **Bookworms > About Bookworms** credits the spine fonts' and Checkout Card's handwriting font's licenses, the table's wood texture, and the desk stamp's model (`Bookworms/Credits.rtf`).
- **Bookworms > Settings…** (Command-comma) has three panes and reopens on the last one: **General** (**Generate while idle**, **Launch at Login**, and **Book details**; see [Library](#library)), **Design Model** (see [Spine design model](#spine-design-model)), and **Hardcover**, which shows the account itself, with sign-in, the bookplate, **Sync now**, and **Sign out**, rather than a sheet over Settings.
- It stays running in the menu bar after its window closes. The **Bookworms** menu bar item, a bookworm lying on a book, shows how many books draw a design, including series members that draw another book's, and the run's count and time left, or its outcome, as the **Spines** tab words them, and offers **Generate Now** or **Stop Generating**, **Open Bookworms**, **Launch at Login**, **Settings…**, which brings the app forward with its Settings window, and **Quit Bookworms**. **Bookworms > Show in Menu Bar**, on by default, shows or hides the item; the **Bookworms** menu stays reachable from the Dock while the item is hidden. The Dock icon's menu offers **Generate Now** or **Stop Generating** and **Sync Library**.
- **Generate while idle** replaces **Generate while charging**. It schedules the same background run with `NSBackgroundActivityScheduler`, which macOS starts when the Mac is idle, with or without a window open. The run starts only while the Mac draws AC power, as a Mac without a battery always does, and otherwise waits for macOS to offer it again. A Mac whose power source can't be read counts as on battery power, and the run also waits when the app can't watch for a switch to battery power. When a MacBook switches to battery power during the run, or its power source can't be read, the run stops, and the next idle run continues. A **Generate now** run keeps going behind other windows and on battery power. While an idle run is in progress, the Spines tab's **Stop** and the **Stop Generating** item in the **Library** menu, the menu bar item, and the Dock icon's menu end it (`GenerationController.canStop`). The next idle run then waits a day from the stop, unless the reader starts a run or relaunches the app first (`GenerationController.nextRunDate(after:hasNewWork:stoppedAt:now:)`).
- In a window wide enough to show more than one stack with books at least three quarters of their largest size, **Library**'s **Stack** shows stacks side by side, at most one for every three books. Each stack holds the next run of books, and the runs differ by at most one book. The stacks rest on one line and scroll together, in 2D and in 3D, as stacks of books on a shelf would. The caption names the centered book of one stack: the stack of a book you tap, or the first stack at first and after a new time or order. Where that stack does not reach the screen's center, the caption names the centered book of the nearest stack that does. VoiceOver reads these books row by row across the stacks.
- **Stack** keeps room at the outer ends of its stacks, so a book you pull out of a stack beside the sidebar or the window's edge stays in full view as it grows toward you and comes out along the way its spine faces (`StackArrangement.layout`). The stacks narrow by only the room that the space beside their books lacks: in the default window, about 26 points at each end in 2D and 41 in 3D, which makes the books about 5 and 9 percent smaller. Larger windows need more room in 3D, where the stack's camera stands farther back, and a window whose stacks already have space to spare beside their books keeps their size. The room never changes how many stacks there are; the window's width alone sets that, in 2D and 3D alike. A book pulled from between two stacks comes out over the stack beside it. With Reduce Motion on, a book opens its details without coming out of the stack, so the stacks keep no room.
- **Share** shows the preview filling the window, with the size and the cube **3D books** button under it, and the settings in an inspector column beside it: 400 points wide, narrowing to 320 in a narrow window. The window's 780-point minimum width leaves room for that column beside the sidebar at its default width; a sidebar widened in the smallest window takes the room, and the inspector column clips at the window's trailing edge until the window widens or the sidebar narrows. The toolbar's **Hide inspector** button (**Show inspector** while it's hidden) and **View > Show Inspector** (Control-Command-I) hide and show it. **Book**, **Pinned books**, and **Compare with** open their lists in popovers beside the inspector, so the image stays in view, each with a search field above the list; choosing a book or a reader closes the popover. Return in the search field chooses the first match, pins or unpins it in **Pinned books**, or in **Compare with** looks up the username you typed when no listed reader has it. Tab moves to the list, where the arrow keys move the highlight and Return chooses it. The **Compare with** list has a **Refresh** button below it, since the Mac has no pull to refresh. **Share** opens the macOS sharing picker, which drops down from the button, here and in the Desk's share sheets. Drag the preview into another app or the Finder to drop it as a PNG file, or use **File > Save Image…** (Command-S) or **Edit > Copy Image**. Click the preview and press Space, click **Quick Look** in the toolbar, or choose **View > Quick Look** (Command-Y) to see it full size. Each copy carries the credit, which is drawn only once the image is dropped, pasted, or saved. Once clicked, the preview also takes Command-C. These commands dim while a sheet that the Share tab opens, such as **Desk video**, covers the image.
- Forms use the grouped style, toasts show at the top of the selected section's content, below the toolbar, and a sheet reports its own failures in an alert, since the main window's toasts sit behind it; sharing shows no "Shared" toast, since the sharing service shows its own result, and Hardcover's approval page opens in the default browser. Each spine color has an inline color well, which opens the Colors panel (see [Spine colors](#spine-colors)), and the **Font** row opens the font picker in a sheet (see [Spine font](#spine-font)). **Stack** and **Shelf** show scroll bars as **Show scroll bars** in System Settings says.
- When a book's details are at least 820 points wide, not counting the sidebar, they stand the 3D book in a column of its own, beside a column with the title and the cards. On the iPhone, the text column takes the screen's trailing half, counting the safe area, up to 640 points (`BookDetailView.textColumnWidth(width:leading:trailing:)`), so on the iPhone Duo's open inner display in landscape the fold falls between the book and the cards. On the Mac, the text column fills up to 640 points and the book takes the rest. Resizing across that width moves the book and restarts its pose. An expanded description stays expanded, but the text jumps by the book's height, about 460 points, since the book leaves or rejoins the top of the scrolling text. Over the details, the toolbar draws no background of its own, so the wall runs unbroken behind the toolbar to the window's top edge.
- On the Desk, a drag picks up a book at once, with no hold, and a click opens it; the pointer shows an open hand over a book and a closed one while it carries one. The scroll wheel zooms toward the pointer, sideways scrolling or Shift with the wheel turns the view about the table under the pointer, as a trackpad's turn does, and an Option-drag turns it about its middle. Right-click a book for **Open**, **Make Share Image**, **Change Cover…** (or **Change Series Cover…**), **Redesign Spine** (or **Redesign Spines**), and **Copy Title**. The pointer finds books from their poses rather than the view, so hovering never wakes a paused desk; a scroll wakes it as a touch does. macOS reports no hovering while the button is down or while books move under a still pointer, so after a drag or click the pointer names no book until it moves, and it finds its book again when the desk rests or wakes. On the dominoes, the scroll wheel zooms toward the pointer as a pinch does, and a drag looks along the path. Scrolls over the sidebar, the toolbar, sheets, and a book's details go to them instead.
- **View > Zoom In** and **Zoom Out** (Command-Plus and Command-Minus) zoom the Desk scene or the dominoes toward the middle of what the toolbar and sidebar leave clear, **Actual Size** (Command-0) returns the Desk scene to its home zoom, and the **Desk** menu offers **Set Up Dominoes** or **Back to Desk**. They are unavailable while another section, a book's details, or a sheet over the Desk scene shows (`DeskCommands`).
- The Desk scene's walls stand between the sidebar and the window's trailing edge as well as below the toolbar, so no book comes to rest under the sidebar (`PileTray`). Tilt and shaking need device motion, which the Mac lacks, so they are off.
- Text that names the device says "Mac", including the export credit "Created with Bookworms for Mac".

The Mac app replaces Bookworms Spines, the retired menu bar companion from the archived `bookworms-macos` repository.

### Tabs

The app opens on **Share**, its main feature; on the Mac, the window can reopen on the section it last showed instead (see [On the Mac](#on-the-mac)). The tab bar reads **Share**, **Library**, **Desk**, and **Spines**. **Library** and **Desk** show your books against a walnut wall, with dark bars in either appearance. On iPhone, that includes the tab bar while either tab shows, so its selected tab reads as a white icon on a dark capsule; the other tabs keep the system tab bar. Behind Library and book details, the walnut wall has a faint wood grain and a seam every 180 points, so it reads as tall panels; with Increase Contrast on, it is plain and a little darker, and its text and the lamp's color are brighter. Elsewhere, buttons, links, and selections use the app's brass accent (see [Look and feel](#look-and-feel)). The **Share** tab's symbol is a keepsake card and the **Spines** tab's a spine with a foil stamp. **Make share image** in a book's details and **Make Share Image** in a book's menus cross-fade the window into the Share tab over about a third of a second; choosing a tab yourself switches at once, as the system does.

On the iPhone Duo, the app fills both displays. Closed, the system moves the tab bar and the bars' buttons to the outer display's trailing edge. Open, the inner display ignores the app's portrait-only orientation, so the app lays out in landscape too: **Share** places its preview beside the settings, and book details at least 820 points wide stand the book beside the text, as on the Mac.

### Library

The **Library** tab shows the books you have read as spines in their saved designs, or in a plain cloth fallback before they have one. As on the Apple TV's default shelf, a book counts as read when it is on your **Read** shelf or has a finish date (`CloudBook.hasBeenRead`); books on **Want to Read** and other unread shelves stay off, and so do current reads. The Hardcover download records the **Read** shelf as `isRead`, and the Apple TV's archive carries the same field.

Under the books, the caption names the centered book (see below). On the iPhone, a footer right under the caption shows the library's status: the time and the number of books it shows, such as "Last 12 months · 39 books", over the sync status, such as "Synced Oct 17 at 2:41 PM", or the date alone for a sync before this year. While a sync runs, it reads "Syncing…", and after a failed sync or library load, **Couldn't sync · Retry**, which syncs again. When the time and the count don't fit on one line, as at the largest text sizes, they take a line each; the footer stops growing at the second accessibility size. At its leading end, **View as** is a segmented control of **Stack** (default) and **Shelf**, and at its trailing end, one glass capsule holds **Find a book** and **View options**; the status centers between them. VoiceOver reads the status as a heading named "Library". The navigation bar hides while the footer shows, so the books run up under the status bar and the Dynamic Island to the screen's top edge; the bar returns for a book's details and for the screens without a library or read books. A time without books keeps the footer. On the Mac, the window's toolbar shows the title **Library** with the sync status as its subtitle, a button in its middle that names the time and the number of books, such as "Last 12 months" and "39", and chooses the time, as Desk's time frame button does, and at its trailing end **View as** as a segmented control, **View options**, and the **Find a book** search field. The menu bar offers the same choices (see [On the Mac](#on-the-mac)). The **View options** symbol, three lines narrowing downward, fills while the time differs from its default; VoiceOver then reads the menu's value as "Filtered". The menu holds, in sections:

- **3D Books**, only for **Stack**.
- **Time**: **Last 12 Months** (default), **This Year**, **Last 5 Years**, **All Time**, and a **Year** menu of the years in which you finished read books, newest first.
- **Sort by** finish date, title, author, or your rating.
- **Remove Filters**, while the time differs from its default, which restores it.

Syncing and the Hardcover account aren't in this menu: **Settings** in the **Spines** tab's toolbar, or the sidebar's account row on the Mac, opens the Hardcover account, whose **Sync now** syncs the library (see [Hardcover sign-in](#hardcover-sign-in)). On the Mac, the menu bar's **Library > Sync Library** and **Library > Hardcover Account…** also do.

The app saves every choice. A new time or order returns to the first book. **Sort by** is the same setting that Desk's options menu shows (see [Desk](#desk)).

**Find a book** finds a book among the books shown, rather than filtering them. The best match is the first book whose title is what you typed, else the first whose title starts with it, else the first whose title or author contains it, ignoring case and accents. On the Mac, the toolbar's search field (**Library > Find Book…**, Command-F) glides the shelf to the best match as you type, the caption names it, and the field lists up to eight matches below it, each with its author. The arrow keys move the highlight through the list, and Return, or a click, opens the highlighted book's details; Return with no book highlighted opens the best match. When the toolbar is too narrow for the field, it shows as a search button. The field stays in the toolbar while a book's details show: the shelf doesn't move as you type, and a book you choose opens once the shown book is back in its place. On the iPhone, **Find a book** opens a sheet, in the system's appearance, that lists the books shown, which a search field narrows; the shelf doesn't move as you type. Choosing a book, or Return in the field for the first one listed, closes the sheet and pulls that book out to its details; with Reduce Motion on, its details open without the flight. On the Mac, in **Stack** and in **Shelf**, the shelf first brings a chosen book that is not at the center of the screen there, wherever it stands, and the book opens half a second later. On the iPhone, the shelf goes to the chosen book behind the closing sheet, so the book opens once the sheet has closed; in **Stack** with Reduce Motion off, the stack instead glides the book to the center after the sheet has closed, and the book comes out half a second later. A book outside the chosen time isn't found; choose **All Time** to search every read book.

On the Mac, Library also works with the keyboard and the pointer:

- **Stack** or **Shelf** takes keyboard focus when Library appears, on a click, or with Tab. The lamp's light behind the stack, or over the shelf, warms while it has focus, instead of a focus ring, and warms more with Increase Contrast on. Up and Down in **Stack**, or Left and Right in **Shelf**, step to the next or previous book, stopping at the ends of the stack; in **Stack**, Left and Right move to the stack beside it. Page Up and Page Down move about a screenful, Home and End go to the first and last book of the stack, and typing a title's first letters goes to the first book whose title starts with them. Return opens the centered book, and Space peeks: the book slides partway out of its place, straight toward you, and back. The keys do nothing while a book's details show.
- While the pointer rests on a book, it eases about 8 percent of the way out of its place, straight toward you along the way its spine faces, with no tilt, and back when the pointer leaves; its tooltip names it, such as "Piranesi — Susanna Clarke". As the pointer moves along the book, the book turns up to 2 degrees about the screen's vertical axis to face it, eased, and returns to rest when the pointer leaves (`PullOutPath.pointerTurn`), as Delicious Library's shelf items face the cursor. Books that scroll under a resting pointer stay in place, and with Reduce Motion on, every book stays still. The iPhone has no pointer, so its books never turn; tilt is off, so nothing stands in for it.
- A book's context menu offers **Open**, **Open in New Window**, **Make Share Image** (which opens the Share tab with the book, as its details' **Share** does), **Change Cover…**, or **Change Series Cover…** for a series that shares a design, **Redesign Spine**, or **Redesign Spines** for such a series, dimmed while a run designs spines, and **Copy Title**.
- **Open in New Window**, Option-click, and Option-Return open a book's details in a window of their own, titled with the book and its author, which macOS restores with the app. The window says so when the library no longer includes the book, and the details' **Share** brings the main window forward on the Share tab.

**Last 12 Months** and **Last 5 Years** reach back from today and have no end: a book counts when you finished it after the same date 12 months or 5 years earlier, by the date in the device's time zone. A finish date after today, which a time zone difference can record, counts too. **This Year** is today's calendar year. Books count by their finish dates (`LibraryTime`): a book you reread shows in every time you finished it in, placed and dated by its newest finish there. Read books without a finish date, such as books on the **Read** shelf with no date recorded, show only under **All Time**. A time without books says so and offers **Show all time**. Desk keeps its own time frame (see [Desk scene](#desk-scene)).

- **Stack** (default) lays the books flat on each other, spines toward you, in a stack that scrolls vertically with ordinary momentum and no snapping. The stack rests on a walnut board, built from **Shelf**'s board; side-by-side stacks on the Mac share one board. The bottom books stand flush with its front edge, so its front face starts right below them, and its top shows only beside them. In 3D, the top shows as the stack's camera sees it wherever the board lies: a sliver at the stack's end, where the bottom books lie across the eye, and more as the stack scrolls back and the board drops below the eye, as the bottom books' top boards do, with a contact shadow beside each bottom book's ends. When you pull the stack past either end, it bounces back as usual, and the stack and its board keep the look they have at that end as they move, so the bottom books never rise above the eye and show their undersides over the board. In 2D, seen straight on, no top or shadow shows. A pool of the lamp's warm light lies behind the stack whose centered book the caption names, so beside other stacks it marks which stack that is, and it glides to another stack when the caption's book moves there. Each spine has its **Shelf** length lying on its side and is thicker than on **Shelf**, a little thicker for a thin book and more for a thick one, so long titles on thin books stay legible; long books are longer and thicker, at the largest size that fits the longest possible book across the stack. Each book lies a little off the middle, by up to 3 percent of the stack's width, and turns on the stack, so its spine faces a little left or right, by amounts that its ID decides; most books lie nearly straight. `Book3DView` draws every book as a plain lit box, which keeps scrolling cheap; the book you tap gains the binding the details draw (see below) as it starts to slide out. Each book lies flat and level on the one below it, touching along its length, as gravity would leave a real stack; no book tilts in the screen's plane. The stack fades out toward the caption. On the iPhone, it runs up under the status bar and ends only at the screen's top edge, where a wash of the wall's color, 85 percent opaque across the status bar and fading out over 8 points below it, hides the lettering under the time and battery, also at accessibility text sizes; on the Mac, it also fades out toward the toolbar.
- In **Stack**, **3D Books** switches between flat spines and 3D books. In 2D, each book is seen straight on and unshaded, and turns by up to about 3 degrees, so only its spine shows, as a flat one. In 3D, each book turns by up to about 7 degrees and is shaded by a light from the screen's upper left, so its top board shows lighter than its underside, as under a room's light, and one camera sees the whole stack in perspective from about where you look at it: as a 100 mm lens on a full-frame camera would frame the stack's area, about 2.3 times its diagonal away, or roughly 30 cm from an iPhone's stack and 60 cm from a Mac window's, level with the area's middle. Books a little above or below the middle show as little of their undersides and top boards as real books would from there. The book there, which the caption names, shows neither its top board nor its underside. A book below the eye shows a sliver of its top board, and a book above it a sliver of its underside, more the farther it lies from the eye, wherever the book next to it on the eye's side is shorter, lies off the middle, or turns; the slivers change smoothly as the stack scrolls. A book also shows the page edges at an end when the eye lies past that end, as it does for a short book turned near the limit, or for the books of a stack to one side of a wide Mac window.
- **Shelf** stands the spines upright, side by side, in a walnut bookcase that scrolls horizontally with ordinary momentum and no snapping. Each spine's height and thickness come from the book's page count and the same per-book length variation the 3D views use (`BookShape`), so long books stand taller and thicker than short ones; thickness starts from a floor of 1.6 cm at the book's scale (`CloudBook.shelfFloor`), thicker than the short shelves in share images and the **Spines** tab, which start from 1.2 cm, so a long title on a thin book letters larger before it shrinks. The bookcase reaches the screen's top edge on the iPhone and the toolbar on the Mac. The spines are as large as the space above the caption allows the tallest possible book to be, 20 points below the shelf above and, on the iPhone, below the status bar, up to a size that still shows about ten books across the screen. The bookcase's back panel, board, and side panels at the start and end of the shelf scroll with the books, while the lamp's light stays fixed to the screen. Each spine is a flat cloth spine with nearly square corners (1 point at most), its long edges darkened a little where the cloth turns onto the boards. `SpineLettering` measures each spine's lettering with Core Text, and `SpineView` draws each line at its measured size. The title starts as tall as its box, 88 percent of the thickness, and keeps to one line unless two lines letter it at least 25 percent larger (`SpineLettering.twoLineGain`). Where one line would letter below 4.5 percent of the spine's length (`oneLineFloor`), which is too small to prefer, it takes whichever is larger. Two lines break between words, where the longer line is shortest, so a one-word title keeps to one line. A two-line title then grows until its letters fill 85 percent of the box (`twoLineFill`), a fifth of the letters' height apart, or until its longer line fills the width: a font's line height leaves room for letters the title may not have, so two lines sized by it would leave much of the box empty. The title takes the width its one line needs first, and a long title keeps at least the width it has beside the author's least share. The author's name takes the rest, at least 24 percent of the spine's length, or the width its surname takes at the name's minimum size if that is wider, and at most 40 percent, and no more than it needs, which a short title leaves to a long name. The name is half the thickness where the title keeps its lines, loses no size to it, and stays at least 1.4 times as large, as beside a short title on a thin spine; otherwise it keeps its minimum, a little over a quarter of the thickness and at least 7 points. Either size is at most 4 percent of the length, so a thick spine gives its extra room to the title. A spine names its author in full wherever it can: at either size, a name too long for its width takes two lines where they fit the thickness, broken before the surname or between two names and centered on each other, and a name that fits neither way at half the thickness tries both again at its minimum. Only a name that fits neither way at either size falls back to the surnames, such as "Le Guin" or "Gaiman, Pratchett", at half the thickness and then at the minimum. As a last resort, the surnames shrink below the minimum (`SpineView.surname(_:)`). Apart from the name's 7-point floor, the lettering's margins and sizes are shares of the spine's length and thickness, so a spine of one shape letters alike at any size. The 3D books, the Desk, and the details draw their spines the same way. The Apple TV letters its spines by a copy of this rule (`LetteringRule` in `tvos/Bookworms/Services/SpineTextMetrics.swift`), with its own safeguards: sizes sit on a half-point grid; a title that the rule would letter below 23 points takes the larger of one line and two filled lines, then a third line; authors letter at 16 points or more, and 4 points smaller than the title where that minimum allows; and a surname that fits no form truncates where this app shrinks it. Keep the rule's shares, its title line rule, and its author forms the same in both apps. A book at least 4.8 cm thick (`SpineView.stamps(_:)`), about 700 pages, has a band pressed into the cloth near its head and its tail, with no ink: a line shadowed on its head side and lit on its tail side, its shadow deeper on lighter cloth. Thinner books have no bands, which would be too small to read.
- The caption shows the title, author, finish date, and your rating of the book nearest the screen's center, in larger text styles on the Mac, with a small badge such as "Read 3 times" for a book you finished more than once, under its place among the books shown, such as "31 of 39". It sits at the bottom of its fixed space, on the footer, so a two-line title takes space above it. On the Mac, which has no footer, it sits 20 points above the window's bottom edge. **Stack** and **Shelf** share that book, so switching between them keeps your place.
- Tap any book in **Stack** or spine in **Shelf** for details. VoiceOver reads each book as a button with its title and author, and activating it opens the details: a 3D book you can turn with a drag, its title, author, and your rating, and **Make share image** (see [Details from Library](#details-from-library)). Below the title, the rest of the book's details follow on one page, which scrolls under the bar and fades into the walnut under a soft scroll edge. A fact the book lacks has no row or card, and no note stands in for it, such as for a review you haven't written. A spine that isn't designed yet still has its **Spine design** card, with **Change cover**, but no note about its placeholder cloth.
  - **Reading record**: when you started and finished the book, with how many times you finished it when that is more than once (such as "Read 3 times" beside the finish date), and how many days that took (counting both days). With catalog cards, it is a cream date-due card with the dates stamped in violet ink, and its inks keep 7:1 on its stock.
  - **About**: the description, with HTML and Markdown removed, cut to six lines. **More** shows the rest in place. The text is selectable.
  - **Readers' rating**: Hardcover readers' average, with stars and the number of ratings. When Hardcover records how many readers gave each half-star rating, bars show them beside the average, with your own rating's bar marked. VoiceOver reads the average, the number of ratings, and your rating.
  - **Details**: in this order, **Series**, **Published** (the publication year), **Length** (the page count, whatever format you read the book in), **Status** (whether you are reading the book now and how far along), **Read as** (the format you read it in, with an audiobook's listening time when Hardcover records it; the page count shows only under **Length**), and **Genres** (up to three). **Series** names the series the book's label names, and the book's place in it, such as "Discworld - Moist von Lipwig" and "2 of 3", or "Book 2" when Hardcover records no size that includes the position. The label names the series you chose for the book, else its shortest series (`SeriesDesigns.shownSeries(of:link:)`, the rule its spine's look follows); a book you took out of every series shows no **Series**. When the book is in two or more series, or its series lists two or more books (`SeriesEntry.opensSeries(of:infos:library:)`), **Series** shows a chevron; tap it to open **Series**, which lists every series Hardcover lists the book in, with the book's place in each. Otherwise it only names the series. The footer under the list says "The checked series names this book on each of your devices that syncs with iCloud." Tap a series to open its books; a book in one series opens straight to them. A check button before each series marks the one the details name; tap another check to make that series the book's series, which saves it as the book's series choice (see [Series designs](#series-designs)) without opening it. Checking the shortest series restores the default. A series' books show in reading order, with your books marked **This book**, **Read**, **Reading**, or **On your shelf**, and the others dimmed, then how many you've read. The list is Hardcover's main books from the last download, one per whole-number position; your copy at a position takes its place, and your books at other positions, such as a novella at 2.5, join in order. Before a download has the series, it lists your own books in it.
  - **Your review**: the review you wrote on Hardcover. With catalog cards, it is a page in New York with a hanging quotation mark and a ribbon.
  - For a finished book, **Pin to Top Five** or **Unpin from Top Five** (see [Share](#share)); while five other books are pinned, the button is disabled and says to unpin one under **Share**, in Top Five's **Pinned** books.
  - The **Spine design** card: the spine lying on its side, 44 to 96 points thick by page count (`CloudBook.slabThickness`), the book's **Series** and **Changes apply to** choices (see [Series designs](#series-designs)), the font, which you can change (see [Spine font](#spine-font)), the colors, which you can change (see [Spine colors](#spine-colors)), the redesign button, **Redesign spine** or, in a series that shares a look or design, **Redesign spines**, and **Change cover** or **Change series cover** (see [Cover choice](#cover-choice)), then which book's design a series shares and when the design was made. After you choose a new cover for a designed book, the redesign button moves to a card just above it that says the spine was designed from the previous cover.
- Tap the book's spine, or within 10 points of it, to scroll to the **Spine design** card; VoiceOver's **Show spine design** action on the book does the same, then moves VoiceOver to the card's heading. A tap elsewhere on the book does nothing. The example profile offers neither (see [Example profile](#example-profile)). The details find the spine where their own book would draw it in the pose on screen, so for a book that the shelf or the Desk scene draws behind the details, the spine they find can differ from the drawn one by a few points.
- The cards are library catalog cards by default: cream card stock with a handwritten heading over a red rule and the hole for the drawer's rod at the foot, field names printed in small capitals, and entries written in pen on printed rules, which run only under writing. The first card is headed with the book's title, written a size larger than other headings, with the author written on the first rule and your rating in pen-ink stars on the next. A title at most a tenth too wide for the card stays on one line, written smaller, down to about 91 percent of its size. A longer title wraps onto as many lines as it needs, 0.96 rule apart, with as many words on each line as fit. It breaks where `Text` would, such as at spaces, after hyphens, dashes, and slashes, and between most Chinese, Japanese, and Korean characters. When one word would be left alone on the last line, the previous line's last word joins it if both fit and that line has another word. A word wider than the card, as at the largest text sizes, shrinks the whole title until it fits, down to the size of the other cards' headings; a word wider still breaks between its letters, and the title keeps its size (`TitleLines`). Each line of handwriting sits on its rule, with descenders crossing it. An entry such as a year or a hex code sits a little off level, by an amount its book decides, while it fits on one line; an entry that wraps stays level. The handwriting is Caveat, downloaded from Google Fonts as for Checkout Card, or Noteworthy at three quarters of the size until it downloads; with **Bold Text** on, Caveat is heavier. The rules are 34 points apart at the default text size, as on the date-due card, and grow with Dynamic Type up to 80 points, at the third accessibility size; at accessibility sizes, the field names stand above their entries, the rating's bars below the average, and the check boxes one above the other. **Readers' rating** writes the average large, with its decimal point set midway between the digits, and draws its bars by hand in pen, hatched, a dash for a rating no reader gave, with your rating's bar in red pencil and "you" written in red along the top of the chart, with a hand-drawn arrow down to that bar. The arrow slants when its course keeps about 4 points from the other bars and all its ink, head included, stays outside the bounds of each of their lines, or else drops nearly straight within its bar's column; only a chart too narrow for even that makes it cross a neighbor. The word and arrow show for every rating. The word ends 2 points from the arrow's tail and never touches the arrow or a bar: a shaft that strays toward the word's ink pushes the word along its baseline. It goes left of the arrow unless it would run off the chart or only the right side leaves room for the arrow. VoiceOver reads your rating after the average and the number of ratings. Each line is a little off true, by amounts its book and bar decide, so the chart is the same on every redraw: each end lands up to a point to either side of its mark, so a line runs a few degrees off course and a bar's sides are seldom parallel; a long line, such as a tall bar's side, also bows and waves gently, up to about two points, while a short one, such as a bar's top or a hatch line, stays nearly straight; nearly every outline end misses its corner, running up to three points past it, so the lines cross, or, less often, stopping up to two points short, so the corner stays open; each line thins where the pen lands and lifts; and the hatch, about 45 degrees and 4 points apart, varies a little in spacing and angle, its lines stopping short of the outline or crossing it a touch. The hatch and dashes are a little lighter than the outlines. With Increase Contrast or Reduce Transparency on, the lines keep one width, and the hatch and dashes are in full ink. On the **Spine design** card, the spine is held by photo corners, the font is lettered in itself, each color is a painted chip on the iPhone or a color well on the Mac with its hex code written beside it, **Changes apply to** is a pair of check boxes, and the creation date is stamped. The cards' buttons, such as **Redesign spine** and **Change cover**, are rubber stamps in the same violet ink and type as the dates, in a rounded frame and a fraction of a degree off level, and each color's reset is a circled arrow stamped in that ink, a few degrees off level. Their ink took unevenly: on the frame, one broad patch with a blotchy edge, at an end, in a corner, along the top or bottom, or across the middle, is up to about 30 percent lighter and a smaller patch is a little lighter; on the type, two small, soft patches, each across parts of two or three letters, are up to about 18 and 11 percent lighter; and a fine grain runs throughout. In a few places away from those patches, the ink pooled: it is a little darker there and feathers raggedly past the edges. On the frame, that is a blotch at one corner, one along the top or bottom that feathers into the frame, and on about half the stamps one along the other edge that feathers out, up to about 1.3 points past the line, the two along the edges tapering into the line at their ends; on the type, it is one side of a few letters, up to about two thirds of a point past them. Each stamp is the same on every redraw, and the stamps on one card are lighter in different places. Pressing one prints a heavier, more even impression with a wash of ink inside the frame. On the Mac, the pointer turns into a pointing hand over an enabled stamp, and the stamp's ink deepens a little: its lighter patches and grain fill in partway. While a run designs spines, the redesign stamp can't be pressed and reads **Designing spines** beside a spinner in its ink; unlike other disabled stamps, whose ink is faded, it keeps full ink so it stays legible. On glass, that row reads **Designing spines** with a system spinner. With Increase Contrast or Reduce Transparency on, the ink is solid, and at accessibility text sizes each symbol stands above its title. **Pin to Top Five** is a stamp on a paper tag held to the wall by a strip of washi tape, as wide as its wider label, **Unpin from Top Five**, so it keeps its size. Tapping it restamps the tag with an old wooden desk stamp, in 0.84 seconds. The stamp, seen from in front and a little above and fully opaque throughout, comes in from above the top of the screen, or of the window on the Mac, straight down onto the tag, over every card and the navigation and tab bars, though under the iPhone's status bar, while the printed label stays as printed, in full ink. After 0.24 seconds the stamp touches the paper, its rubber face covering the label and its shadow darkening under it, and on that frame the new label lands under it, unpressed, with a soft thunk synthesized on the device and, on an iPhone, a soft haptic tap. The rubber gives a little as the stamp presses for a moment, then the stamp lifts straight up and out of the screen the way it came. Once it clears the label, the new label's fresh ink soaks in: its heavier strokes, landed visibly thinner and a little darker, spread out by up to about 0.9 points, where ink pooled, with soft edges to the ordinary impression over the next 0.45 seconds, while faint ink barely moves. If the tap leaves the pin as it was, the stamp lands the same label again. A tap before the stamp touches the paper pins or unpins again, and the label lands for the result; a tap after it pins or unpins, and the tag restamps again once the label settles. VoiceOver reads the new label at once. With Reduce Motion on, or at accessibility text sizes, where the label stacks its symbol above its title and stands far taller than the stamp's rubber face, the label changes at once, without the stamp; with Increase Contrast or Reduce Transparency, the stamp comes down and the solid label changes under it, without the soak-in. Either way, the thunk and the haptic tap play. A pin changed elsewhere, such as under **Share**, changes the label at once and silently. The thunk mixes with other audio, follows the silent switch, and stays off while **Sounds** is off in **Desk options**. On glass, the label changes at once, without a sound. VoiceOver reads each field as one element, such as "Published, 1993", and reads the handwriting as plain text.
- **Book details**, on the iPhone in **Settings** under the Hardcover account, which only the gear in the **Spines** tab's toolbar opens, and in **Settings > General** on the Mac, chooses **Note cards**, the default, or **Glass cards** for every book's details. Each device keeps the choice. In any build, `-detailsNoteCards NO` or `YES` shows glass or catalog cards for that launch; the argument outranks the saved choice, so while it is passed, **Book details** can't switch them. On glass, nothing in the details is paper. The title, author, and your rating are printed on the walnut above the cards. The reading record and your review are full-width glass cards in plain type, with the dates written out, such as "Mar 12, 2025". Between them, the description, the readers' rating with your rating's bar in gold, and the facts sit in two columns of cards: the description, the genres, and a rating with bars fill their rows; the other cards pair up in order, and when an odd number sit between two full-row cards, the first fills a row alone, so the grid has no gaps. Paired cards share a height. At accessibility text sizes, every card fills its row. VoiceOver reads each card as one element.
- Once the details' book arrives, it floats gently, as the Apple TV's Book Wall floats a book in its details: it drifts up and down by about 15 points over a 40-second cycle, from side to side by about 4 points over 48 seconds, and turns up to 2 degrees each way over 60 seconds. The float eases in over its first four seconds from the pose the book arrived in, continues while you drag or the book coasts, and pauses while the book is scrolled out of view, then resumes where it paused. A book that flies back to the shelf, the Desk scene, or the Dominoes leaves from where it floats. With Reduce Motion on, the book holds still.

#### Details from Library

With Reduce Motion off, tapping a book in **Stack** or a spine in **Shelf** pulls the book off the shelf and floats it into its details, as the Desk scene lifts a book (see [Details from the Desk scene](#details-from-the-desk-scene)) and the Apple TV's Book Wall pulls one out. In **Stack**, a book that is not at the center of the screen first glides there, over half a second, so it slides out in full view, clear of the stack's ends, and the caption shows it. With Reduce Motion on, the details push with a zoom from the book.

- The flight takes 1.1 seconds, about as long as the Desk scene's. Each part of it eases over its own share of that time, and each starts before the one before it ends, so the book never stops:
  - For the first third of a second, the book slides out of its place. It comes straight toward you, growing by 15 percent; on **Stack**, it comes out along the way its spine faces, so a turned book also moves a little toward the side it faces; in 3D, it grows about its middle, and in 2D, it moves down the screen as it grows, so it stays clear of the book above, before it starts to rise. It casts a soft shadow on the shelf, and its faces take on the shading the details give their book. The gap where it stood is shaded by the books beside it, more as the book leaves it, so no shadow shows around a book that still fills its place. On **Shelf**, those books stay put. On **Stack**, the books above the pulled book shift up, by 4.5 points next to it and up to 6 points farther out, each starting 30 milliseconds after the book nearer the gap, with a slight overshoot and settle, like a nudge passing along a Newton's cradle; they stay level and hold their places while the book is out. The books below it stay put, resting on the board. Once the book is back in its gap, they snap shut the same way, nearest first, and rebound a fraction of a point before they settle.
  - As the slide ends, the book floats to where the details show their book, centered in the same 460-point stage, and turns from its spine to the same three-quarter pose; a book from **Stack** also stands up from lying on its back and straightens from its turn. A book from **Stack** first comes on toward you lying level and stands up only as the shelf starts to dim, so it never tilts across the books it has just left.
  - About halfway through, a walnut veil, the details' backdrop, starts to darken the shelf, and the lamp glow that the details show behind their book comes up. On **Shelf**, the shelf's lamp lights the book as it lit the spine, at the same strength, until the veil hides the shelf.
- `Book3DView` draws the flying book, as the details draw theirs. It starts exactly as the book lay: with the spine's size, lettering, and light, and on **Stack** with the book's turn, and in 3D with its shading. A book from **Shelf** starts as its flat spine; as the book turns, its corners square up and the light's shading, the rounded edges, and the cover's fold onto the spine fade in. Nothing fades, and stacked books stay solid: a book on **Stack** first slides straight out toward you, along the way its spine faces. The stack draws this slide itself, among the books around it, and the book covers them and any stack beside it from the tap on, as a book coming out in front of them would; in 2D, the book also moves down the screen as it grows, so its top edge stays on the top edge of its place, clear of the book above. Only once it is clear does the book rise and stand up, in front of the stack, and the return reverses the same steps, so it slides back into its gap the same way. The books around it keep the room they made while it is out, even though nothing then holds them up, so the book slides back without touching them, and then snap shut around it. The stack draws each book exactly as the flight draws it at rest, in both styles, so the flight's first and last frames lie on the stacked book. In 3D, the pulled book and the flight see it from the stack's camera as it lies in its place, not where it has slid to, and keep doing so if the stack moves while the book is out. A book that the pointer has eased out of its place starts from there, and the return ends there while the pointer still rests on it, so the book never snaps back into its place first. The flight then takes the details' size and keeps the spine's thickness, so the book lands in its details in the shape it had in its place: Stack's and Shelf's spines are thicker than the book's page count makes it (`CloudBook.librarySpineRatio(upright:)`), and the details that Library opens, flown or pushed, draw their book in that proportion (`BookDetailView.thicknessRatio`). Details opened anywhere else draw the book's own proportion (`BookShape`). In 3D the flight moves from the stack's camera to the one that sees the details' book, while the light stays at the screen's upper left as the book stands up. The details fade in as the book lands. The book scrolls with them, a drag turns it as it turns the pushed details' book, and **Change cover** retextures it.
- While the details show, the bar shows **Back** and **Make share image** in place of the title, and the shelf and its footer ignore touches. On the Mac, the window takes the book's title, with its author as the subtitle, so the **Window** menu and Mission Control name it, as they do for details from Desk and for pushed details. **Back**, Escape, **Go > Back** (Command-[), or a swipe in from the screen's left edge, fades the details out and flies the book back the way it came in about a second, starting where the details showed it, and slides it into its gap. On the iPhone, the bar hides as the return starts, which moves the fading details up, but not the book. **Shelf** stays scrolled where it was. **Stack** scrolls behind the details to center the book once they show, and again as **Back** starts, so the book always returns to the middle of the screen in full view, and the caption shows it. A book you spun turns the short way to its spine from where it stands, even if it was still coasting. **View options** works again as the book flies back, while the shelf itself ignores touches until the book is back in its gap. **Back** during the opening flight reverses it from where the book is. Tapping the **Library** tab again does not close the details.
- On the iPhone, a light tap of the haptic engine marks the book leaving its place and seating in it again; on the Mac, a trackpad that supports it taps as the book seats.
- **Make share image** opens the Share tab with the book and keeps the details for your return. Switching tabs any other way, changing **View as**, the time, the order, or **3D Books** while the book flies back, or a library update that takes the book off the shelf closes the details at once and puts the book back without the flight.
- VoiceOver reads the details and **Back** instead of the shelf while they show, and the escape gesture goes back. The flight has been checked only in the Simulator; check its smoothness on a device.

### Cover choice

**Change cover** on a book's details opens the covers of the book's Hardcover editions, most-read edition first, laid on the walnut table with soft shadows, the current cover outlined and marked by a ribbon that comes out from under its foot. Each cover shows its edition's format and year, with "Hardback" for Hardcover's "Hardcover" format so it doesn't read as the service, and VoiceOver reads it as, for example, "Cover from 2019 Paperback edition". In a series that shares a look or design, the sheet is titled **Series cover**, names the book whose cover it changes, and says "Used for every book in" the series. On the Mac, the sheet takes the form size. Choosing a cover saves it; **Use default cover** returns to the cover Bookworms picks from your library.

- The picker lists up to 60 editions with images and leaves out repeated images, non-HTTPS links, images under 300 pixels on the shorter side, and nearly square art such as audiobook covers. A cover that does not load cannot be chosen.
