- Finding covers needs this device's Hardcover sign-in, which reads the editions with the same read-only scopes as the library. Signed out, the sheet explains this and offers **Connect Hardcover**. If Hardcover rejects the login, for example after it expired or was revoked, the sheet offers **Reconnect Hardcover**. Either closes the picker and opens the Hardcover sheet in its place. **Use default cover** works in these states and when Hardcover cannot be reached.
- The choice is saved to iCloud as a `CoverChoiceV1` record for the shown account (see [iCloud contract](#icloud-contract)), so another iPhone on the same iCloud account shows it after its next reload. Choosing the default cover deletes the record. If iCloud rejects the change, or the shown library no longer includes the book, for example after signing in to another account, the sheet says so and nothing changes. Without an iCloud account it says "Sign in to iCloud on this iPhone.", and without a connection "Bookworms couldn't reach iCloud. Check your connection and try again." (`SpineWorker.iCloudMessage(for:)`); the color card, the font alert, and the series choices use the same sentences. The footer under the covers says Bookworms saves the choice in iCloud only while the signed-in library has iCloud.
- The chosen cover replaces the default everywhere the iPhone shows the book: details, share images, the 3D views, and spine generation. If the chosen image stops loading, the default cover shows instead. The 3D views keep a book's textures until a hidden Desk scene or domino chain gives them up after a memory warning, and build the book again when its spine design, reading wear, or covers change (`BookFactory.Parts`), at the Desk scene's next drop or the chain's next setup. A new cover appears there then. The exception is a book whose details you opened from the Desk scene: it shows the new cover at once, in its details and back in the pile.
- The spine keeps its current design until the next run, which rechecks the book because its chosen cover changed. A book that draws its series' look keeps the look through that run, which saves a copy of the look for the book; only the redesign button, with **Changes apply to** set to **Whole series**, remakes the look from the new cover, unless the run's model tier is better than the look's and the book is the one that upgrades it (see [Series designs](#series-designs)). After a change, the details offer the redesign button, which starts a run that designs the book first. If a run ends without a new design, the details show why, or that the run left the spine unchanged. In a series that shares a look or design, the button reads **Change series cover** and chooses the cover of the book the series' spine comes from, and the redesign gives the whole series the new design (see [Series designs](#series-designs)).

### Spine colors

The **Spine design** card at the bottom of a book's details shows the spine, the book's series choices (see [Series designs](#series-designs)), its font, and its background, text, and accent colors. On the iPhone, tap a color, its name, or its hex value to change it in the system color picker, which offers no opacity. For VoiceOver, each color is a button named for its role, with its hex value, such as "Background color, #1A2B3C". On the Mac, each color's row has an inline color well that opens the Colors panel, also without opacity, and the spine follows it as you pick. A color that differs from the generator's has a reset at its row's trailing edge, a circled arrow, stamped in violet ink on catalog cards and a plain symbol on glass, that VoiceOver names for its role, such as "Reset Background color".

- The card's spine shows the picked colors at once, while you drag in the picker. On the iPhone, the picker's sheet is as tall as the picker's content, down to its current color and saved colors, as the picker reports it, and you can lower it to half the screen. While it shows, the card holds only the spine, shrunk to three quarters of its size with its lettering unchanged, a small copy of the book's cover beside it, the contrast note, if any, and the row of the color you're picking, with its reset, and the details scroll the card's heading to just below the bar. At the default text size on an iPhone 17 Pro without the contrast note, those stay above the taller sheet; where the taller sheet would cover them, as on the iPhone SE or with the contrast note, the sheet opens at half height, and you can raise it. The sheet doesn't dim the details, so you can tap the reset while you pick. The picker's eyedropper can sample the cover: drag it over the cover and lift, and the picker comes back with that color. The picker closes when you leave the card: when you tap **Back** or **Make share image**, switch tabs, or open a sheet from the details, such as **Series**. Closing the picker brings back the card's other rows. One second after the last change, or when you leave the details, the app saves them, and the details' 3D book, **Stack**, **Shelf**, and the share images show them. A book lifted from the Desk scene or Dominoes lands back with the new colors; the 3D views show other changed books, such as one whose details pushed with Reduce Motion, after their next drop or set-up.
- The colors go to the book's `SpineDesignV1` record in iCloud, or to the preview folder in preview mode, with the same font, hashes, and creation date (see [iCloud contract](#icloud-contract)). Apple TV builds that draw companion designs show the cloth and text colors the next time they read designs, such as when the app becomes active. The Apple TV's Book Wall draws the accent color, including one you edited, only in the headbands and a five-star book's sprayed page edges, never on the spine; it also checks the color and copies it into series looks. If the save fails, the card says why and keeps the colors on screen, and leaving the details tries once more. If another device redesigned the spine or changed its font in the meantime, the colors still apply to the design iCloud holds.
- For a book that draws its series' look with **Changes apply to** set to **Whole series**, the colors go to the series' `SeriesLookV1` record instead, with the same font and creation date and a new edit date, and replace whatever colors the look had, so every book in the group shows them. If another device remade the look or changed its font in the meantime, the colors still apply to the look iCloud holds, and the card shows the result after the next reload. A book in a group without a look saves them to the source's design, so the whole series shows them too. With **This book only**, the colors go to the book's own design.
- When the text contrasts less than 4.5:1 with the background, which every reader of the design requires, the card's spine still shows the pair, a note under it asks for a lighter or darker color, shortened to "The text needs more contrast to save." while the picker shows, and nothing is saved or shown elsewhere. The app never adjusts a color you picked.
- A color's reset appears while that color differs from the generator's, and restores only that color, saving it at once. For a series look, those are the generator's colors of the design the look was made from, while that design was made no later than the look was made or last remade and keeps the look's generator, weight, case, and, unless you chose the look's font, its font; a source redesigned on its own afterward offers no reset, and neither does a look remade from a new design before looks recorded when they were remade (`remadeAt`), until the redesign button remakes it again. A reset keeps a font you chose. Otherwise, such as for a look the Apple TV made, the card offers no reset.
- Each saved color change, color reset, series choice, and cover choice registers with the window's undo manager as **Change Color**, a reset named for its color such as **Reset Background Color**, **Change Series Choice**, or **Change Cover**, so **Edit > Undo** and **Redo** on the Mac, and shaking on the iPhone, restore the previous choice and save it again. A color change registers as the library shows it, a second after the last pick, without waiting for iCloud's save; a save that fails before the library shows the colors clears these undo steps. Undoing a cover needs that cover among the editions the picker listed.
- Generation keeps your colors. A run leaves a recolored spine as it is, even after a generator or metadata change, or when the cover's image changes, such as a new default cover from Hardcover, until you choose another cover with **Change cover**. The next run, such as the one the redesign button starts, then designs the spine from the new cover with new colors. A series look you recolored keeps its colors and type through ordinary runs, even after **Change series cover**, until the redesign button, with **Changes apply to** set to **Whole series**, remakes it. The iPhone compares the chosen cover with the one at the book's last check on this iPhone; a book it has not checked yet keeps your colors. A design that generation copies from another book's design records the generator's colors, not yours; a copy of a series look records the look's colors.

### Spine font

The **Font** row of the **Spine design** card names the spine's font, set in that font. Tap it, or click it on the Mac, to open the font picker. It lists the families the design tried, best match first (at most six, `SpineChoice.candidates`), after the family the spine shows when that isn't one of them, then **Other**. A design saved before designs recorded the families they tried lists only its own family. The picker hides Google's Noto families made for other writing systems or number sets, such as Noto Sans Old Hungarian, Noto Sans Mayan Numerals, or Noto Serif Tangut: every Noto family but Noto Sans and Noto Serif with no qualifier other than Mono, Display, or a width or weight word, such as Noto Sans Mono or Noto Serif Display (`AppModel.listsInPicker(_:)`). A design may still use a hidden family, and the picker then lists it as the spine's family. Each row sets the book's title in its family, as the spine letters it, with the family's name in small italics below, and a check marks the spine's family. A row shows a placeholder, and can't be chosen, while its face downloads; a family that can't download, or that lacks a glyph of the title or author of any book that shares the spine's design, so that its series keeps one font, is dimmed and can't be chosen (`AppModel.sharedLettering(of:uppercase:)`). **Other** lists the Google Fonts families that spines may use, the font atlas's families minus those new designs never use, so no choice draws a slanted spine, and minus the hidden Noto families, most popular first, and searches them by name, showing the 50 most popular families without a search and the first 50 whose names contain it with one, each in its own font.

- A choice shows on the card's spine, and everywhere else the spine appears, at once, and keeps the spine's colors. On the iPhone, the picker opens over the lower half of the screen, so while it shows, the details scroll the card to the top, as they do for a color. The picker stays open, so you can compare families on the card's spine, and takes no other choice until the save ends.
- The font goes to the book's `SpineDesignV1` record in iCloud, or to the preview folder in preview mode: the family, its instance nearest the design's weight, and the family's system typeface, with a `reader-font` token (see [iCloud contract](#icloud-contract)). Apple TV builds that draw companion designs draw it like any design's font. If the family can't download, an alert says so and nothing changes. If the save fails, an alert says why and the font stays on screen until the next reload. If another device redesigned the spine or changed its colors in the meantime, the font still applies to the design or look iCloud holds. When the design or look is gone from iCloud, such as after another device moved the book out of its series, the app reloads and applies the font once more to what the book draws then; only if that fails too does an alert say "Another device is changing this spine. Try again in a few minutes."
- A book that draws its series' look, with **Changes apply to** set to **Whole series**, changes the look's font instead, and a book in a group without a look changes the source's design, as colors do (see [Spine colors](#spine-colors)).
- Generation keeps your font as it keeps your colors (see [Spine colors](#spine-colors)): a run leaves a book's own design in your font until you choose another cover with **Change cover**, and a series look until the redesign button remakes it. The redesign button replaces either. A font choice registers no undo step, and the card offers no reset. Choosing the family the design tried first sets the spine in that family again, but the design keeps its `reader-font` token, so the spine stays pinned as your choice, as above, and a run never replaces it.

### House of Leaves

Mark Z. Danielewski's *House of Leaves* prints the word "house" in blue, and Bookworms does the same for that book alone. Wherever the app shows the book's own text, each "house" is blue: its title in **Library** captions, its details, the Share tab's **Book** list, and the Spines tab's progress, its description and review, its spine lettering in every view, including the 3D books and a coverless front, and every share image that letters it.

- The app recognizes the book by its author, Danielewski, and its title, "House of Leaves" in any case, ignoring a subtitle after a colon or a dash and an edition note in parentheses or brackets, so every edition counts. No other book changes, including one with "house" in its title.
- The word counts in any case and with a plural or possessive ending, such as "HOUSE", "houses", or "house's", but not inside another word, such as "household" or "lighthouse".
- The blue starts from the novel's #6570AE and keeps its hue. It moves in lightness only as far as it must to reach WCAG 4.5:1 and APCA Lc 60 against the lightest ground behind it, so each ground gets its own blue: #5F6AA8 in light system lists, #A9B7FA on the walnut wall and in dark lists, #ACBAFD on the details' cards, and in share images a blue fitted to the image's color theme, such as #AFBDFF for a neutral one, a little lighter for a review's title beside the glow behind the cover. Spine lettering takes a blue fitted to the spine's cloth, such as #6570AE itself on a white cloth.
- A faint white glow sits behind a blue that is darker than its ground, such as on a light or yellow cloth, where it lightens the ground beside the letters and adds contrast. A blue lighter than its ground, as on the walnut wall, has no glow, because there a glow would take contrast away.
- On a cloth that no blue reads on, such as a mid-blue one, the word takes the spine's lettering color.
- VoiceOver reads the text unchanged. Cover images keep their own lettering.

### Desk

The **Desk** tab shows your books in a 3D scene, on a wooden desk under a lamp at night. It opens on the [Desk scene](#desk-scene), which drops the last 12 months' books, a year's or month's, or up to 300 books, onto the desk as a physics toy to throw around, and shares the pile as an image or a video. **Dominoes** turns the books on the desk into [Dominoes](#dominoes): they stand themselves up in the order you finished them, cobwebs string across long gaps between finishes, a push topples them, and they gather into a face-up spread to share as a video or an image. **Back to desk** returns to the Desk scene with the books where they lie. Desk shows the read books, as Library does, and current reads, which the Desk scene marks with ribbons under **All Time**.

- The bar shows the time frame button in its middle; at its leading edge, **Dominoes** in the Desk scene, which is unavailable until the last book of a drop has landed, or **Back to desk** in the dominoes, and the Desk scene's **Arrange** menu, which holds **Drop again** and the shapes, or the dominoes' **Set up again**; and, at its trailing edge, the **Desk options** menu and **Share**: the Desk scene's image or, once the dominoes have gathered, their video and image. On the Mac, **Dominoes** and **Back to desk** show their names beside their symbols. Without a library or read books, the bar shows none of these.
- **Desk options** holds the scene's options (**Camera** for the dominoes, **Sounds**, and the Desk scene's drop by period) and **Sort by** for the Desk scene. **Sort by** is available only where its order changes what the Desk scene shows: with **Reduce Motion** on, it orders the tidy stacks, and when the time frame has more books than the desk drops, it picks which books drop. A time frame of 151 to 300 books keeps **Sort by** dimmed even though the order picks which 150 books a share image shows, because the pile looks the same either way. Otherwise every book drops, at once or by period, and the order changes only which wave a book falls in, so the menu dims **Sort by**. Dominoes stand in finish order whatever **Sort by** says, so the menu offers no **Sort by** for them. **Sort by** is the same setting as Library's, so a change on either tab shows on both.
- The time frame button chooses the time frame that both scenes share, apart from Library's time (see [Desk scene](#desk-scene)).
- A sync starts no drop while Desk is the selected tab or a book's details stay open (`DeskBookHold`). Books it adds or removes reach the scenes after you leave Desk and come back, which starts their drop. Its changes to the books on the desk, such as a new cover, apply at once and start no drop, and a time frame or order you choose shows the latest library. A desk without books takes a sync's books at once, and another account or the example profile replaces the desk's books at once. A sign-in to the account whose iCloud library already shows waits like a sync.
- While a lifted book's details show, **Back** and **Make share image** replace the bar's controls.
- Leaving Desk for another tab stops motion, haptics, and sounds and takes the scene's view down, so nothing simulates, draws, or runs a frame loop while another tab shows; the scene keeps its books, so the pile or the chain is as you left it when you return. A memory warning while Desk is hidden frees the books and textures, and a paused desk's still, and they drop or stand again on return. The scenes draw in one `ARView` that a UIKit container adds when it enters a window and removes when it leaves (`DeskSceneView`): SwiftUI defers removing a `RealityView` from a hidden tab until the tab shows again, which kept RealityKit's frame loop running out of sight, and a view torn down that late left the next one drawing nothing. The SwiftUI gestures that pick books lie in a layer over the view, since gestures on the hosted view would not fire, and the scenes find the touched book with their own rays. The Desk scene's rays pass through its invisible walls, since the camera stands outside the near wall.
- Once nothing in the scene that shows has moved for 10 seconds (`SceneRest.delay`), the desk pauses: a still of the view takes its place and the view comes down, so nothing draws or runs a frame loop. RealityKit has no public way to pause an `ARView` or lower its frame rate, and a hidden or empty one keeps drawing. Both scenes count the rest the same way, through the stage they share (`DeskStage.rest`). The pile rests while no book is held, flies to or from its details, waits to drop, or falls, no camera moves, **Tilt** isn't following the phone, and no period's name glows. The dust doesn't count: once nothing has moved for 6 seconds, no new motes drift into the beam, so the last have faded out before the pause and the still holds none. Returning to the Desk tab counts the rest afresh, so the dust drifts in again before the next pause. A touch, **Drop again**, a new time frame or order, a library update, a late cover, a new view size, **Tilt**, the power saving, **Dominoes**, or, on the Mac, a scroll or a zoom command wakes it at once (`DeskScenes.wake()`): the view returns over the still, which stays until the view has drawn, so the swap doesn't show, and the dust drifts back in over a few seconds, and a tap or hold on a paused desk wakes it and finds its book as on a live one, waiting up to half a second for the view to draw. A pause that cannot happen, such as while the share sheet covers the pile, waits another 10 seconds of rest. The dominoes rest only once the books lie gathered under the heading and the recording is written, since the recording, the pointing hand, and the clock need every frame until then; no book may fly, no camera, lamp, or torn cobweb may move, and every book must lie still. A touch, a pinch or drag, a book's details, **Camera**, **Set up again**, **Back to desk**, a new time frame, a late cover, **Reduce Motion**, or, on the Mac, a scroll wakes the dominoes the same way. **Share**'s **Image** shares the recording's last frame, which needs no view, and wakes the view only when there is no recording. The desk doesn't pause while a lifted book's details show over it: scrolling the details moves the book, and a waking view would take several frames to follow.
- The two scenes share the view, the desk and its lamp (`DeskStage`), and the books' meshes and textures (`BookFactory`), through `DeskScenes`; the scene that does not show holds no books. **Dominoes** and **Back to desk** swap the scenes between two frames and hand over the books as they lie, with how they move, and the camera's view, so the books, the desk, the lamp, and the view go on without a cut, and the receiving scene builds its books without drawing a texture again.
- Tips teach the gestures that nothing on screen shows, each once (`DeskTips`, with TipKit): **Pick up a book** at the Desk scene's first rest, pointing at the book nearest the middle; **Look around the desk**, on zooming and turning, at a rest after you have thrown two books; and **Push the first book**, by the pointing hand, the first time a chain waits for its push. On the Mac, they speak of drags, scrolls, and clicks. A tip goes for good once you use its gesture or close it. The Desk scene's tips show only while the desk rests, over its still, and go when it wakes; the push tip shows only while the chain waits, goes while you move the camera or a book's details show, and then points at the hand again; on the Mac, a scroll or menu zoom takes it down for the rest of that wait. A switch between the Desk scene and the dominoes takes either scene's tip down, since the tips' anchors lie over both scenes (`DeskTipAnchors`). The views tell TipKit when the desk rests or wakes and when the chain stands or starts, so TipKit evaluates nothing per frame. The app configures TipKit at launch; in Debug builds, `-BookwormsTips reset` forgets which tips have shown, and `-BookwormsTips show` shows every tip whatever its rules.

### Desk scene

The Desk scene, where the **Desk** tab opens, drops up to 300 books, the first in **Sort by**'s order, onto a table as RealityKit rigid bodies, in waves of 16, where they pile up. It also stops at 250 liters of books (`PileLayout.maximumVolume`), so a library of long books drops fewer; a typical library's 300 books take about 240 liters.

- While a new pile gets its books ready (their meshes, textures, fonts, and covers, and the room's light), a small red cloth-bound book with gilt bands spins and tumbles wildly over the table, above the words **Setting up** (`SpinningBook`). Core Animation turns it outside the main thread, so it keeps spinning while the books build; with **Reduce Motion** on, it holds still and pulses. It fades in only after a quarter second, so a new drop that reuses its books, such as **Drop again**, shows none, and once it has shown, it stays until the first wave can come into view and fades out as those books fall in (`PileLayout.entryDelay(of:seenBy:)`). A new time frame or a filter that changes the pile's books shows it again while their books get ready.
- A second after launch, unless Desk already shows, the app builds the desk's scenes, table, and lamp, and a 3D view, so the Desk tab opens without building them (`DeskScenes.prewarm()`). The dominoes' scene waits until **Dominoes** first needs it. When the desk's view leaves the screen, as for another tab or while the desk rests, it waits out of the window for its return, since making a view stalls the main thread for several frames (`SceneWindowView.dropView()`); a view out of a window draws nothing.
- Every falling book starts out of view and falls in past the top of the screen, however you have zoomed or turned the camera: each drop starts high enough over the table (`PileLayout.spawnHeight(over:)`), about 2.4 m on an iPhone, that every book of it starts wholly above the top of any view the pile's camera can show. This holds for later waves, a drop by period, **Drop again**, and books a library update adds.

- On **Desk**, a button in the middle of the navigation bar shows the time frame next to a calendar icon and the book count next to a books icon, such as "2025" and "39". For **All Time**, it shows the years the books span, and when the pile drops only its first books, how many it shows, such as "2009–2026" and "300 of 412". On the iPhone, it is a small tinted capsule, without glass, so the bar adds no blur over the scene, and where the bar leaves it too little room between its buttons, it drops the calendar icon, then the books icon, then the count, so a long span such as "2009–2026" shows alone. On the Mac, it has one fixed size: the toolbar builds a button's whole menu for each size it could show, each time the button joins the bar, which stalls a lifted book's return, so a window too narrow for the button moves it into the toolbar's overflow (**»**) menu instead. For VoiceOver, it is one button named **Time frame**, with the period and count as its value, such as "2025, 39 books"; how VoiceOver reads it has not been checked. Its text grows up to the largest standard text size; at accessibility sizes, touch and hold it to see it larger. Tap it to choose **Last 12 Months**, **All Time**, or a year: each year with finished books is a row that opens to **All of** that year and its months. The menu lists the three newest years and folds the older ones into **Earlier Years**, unless only one is older. The row that holds the period on the desk carries a checkmark. Until you choose one, the pile shows the last 12 months, which reach back from today as Library's do (see [Library](#library)). The year and month lists include only periods with finished books. Choosing one drops that period's finished books in a new pile, as **Drop again** does. The Desk scene and Dominoes share the choice, apart from Library's time, and the app saves it. If the last 12 months or the saved year has no finished books in the current library, the pile uses the newest year instead, and in place of a saved month without them, the newest month.

- Each book has the size of a real book, and page count sets it apart as on the Apple TV's Book Wall (`BookShape.size(pages:coverAspect:variety:)`). Length grows from 17 cm, for books of up to 200 pages, to 23 cm at 800 pages, and thickness is a real hardcover's: 6.5 mm for the boards, the case, and the spine's round, plus 0.0575 mm a page, the paper printers' spine formulas give for book stock, so a stack of books is as tall as the real books would stand. As its ID decides, each book then gets none, half, or all of 22 percent more length, so books of similar length still differ; variety never changes thickness. A book is at least 3 times as long as it is thick, as the thickest real hardcovers are, but never longer than 28 cm. A 120-page novella is 17 to 21 cm long and 1.2 cm thick, a 350-page novel 19 to 23 cm long and 2.8 cm thick, a 900-page doorstopper 23 to 28 cm long and 6 cm thick, and a 1,400-page book 27 to 28 cm long and 8.8 cm thick. Width follows the cover's aspect ratio (2:3 without a cover), rounded to 0.05. Length rounds to 1 cm, thickness to 4 mm, and width to 5 mm, so books of one size share a mesh. A hardcover's boards overhang its pages by 3.5 mm and its spine is rounded. The covers' edges are beveled, so they catch the light. The front shows the cover, cropped to the front board, which ends where the spine starts to curve, so a little narrower than on its details screen; a hardcover's board edges show the cover's border, as a wrapped jacket does. The spine shows the saved design rendered by `SpineView`, at one scale across its curve, and the back is the spine's cloth color. Physics treats each book as a box of its size.
- Every book draws as a hardcover (`CloudBook.binding`), whatever your Hardcover edition is, with a satin jacket that has a faint, soft sheen. The lamp hangs nearly behind your view, so a book that tilts toward you, such as one resting on another, reflects it across its whole cover; the sheen stays faint enough that the cover's dark ink stays dark (see [Check the covers' finish under the lamp](#check-the-covers-finish-under-the-lamp)). The code keeps paperbacks, with flush covers, a flat spine, and a matte card finish, and an edition-based binding that no view uses (`CloudBook.likelyBinding`): the hardcover or paperback that your edition's format (`CloudBook.editionFormat`) names, such as "Library Binding" or "Mass Market Paperback", and otherwise a stable guess that makes hardcovers of three in four books first finished within a year of publication and two in five other books.
- Page edges show fine leaves and yellow with the time since you first finished the book, fully aged after 15 years, so a reread looks the same in every time frame. A five-star book's edges are sprayed in its spine's accent color. A book you are reading has a red ribbon bookmark that comes out of its tail at your progress and droops toward the back cover, so it shows only while the book lies front cover up, as gravity would let it hang. A year or month holds only the books you finished then, so ribbons show under **All Time**. After a library sync, the next drop, such as **Drop again**, shows new progress, ratings, and finish dates.
- A lamp high above the desk, over your right shoulder, casts a warm pool of light on the pile. It lights the covers, spines, and page edges that face you, and the books' shadows fall up and to the left of them. Full light spans the middle of the pool, and the table fades into a dark room beyond it, which hides the repeats of its wood texture. A dim room drawn in code, with the lamp's glow on the wall behind you, lights the shadows and shows faintly in the hardcovers' jackets. Dust motes drift slowly through the beam around its axis, where its light is full: about 100 faint, soft specks of its warm light, slender irregular flecks about 2 to 5.5 mm long drawn from nine shapes, each at its own angle, turning slowly as it fades in and out over 2 to 3 seconds (`DeskStage`), except with **Reduce Motion** on.
- In Low Power Mode, or while the device's thermal state is serious or critical, the desk saves power until the condition passes: no new dust drifts into the beam, so the motes fade out over the next 3 seconds, and the books lose the soft shadows right under them (`DeskStage.setReducesEffects(_:)`, `BookFactory.setGroundingShadows(_:under:)`). It applies to both scenes, and so to the dominoes' recordings of the live view; pile share images, which render offscreen, keep every effect. In Debug builds, `-BookwormsReducedEffects YES` saves power for the whole launch.
- Most books land front cover up, about nine in ten, and a little fewer with **Drop by month**, while a few land face down, on an edge, or leaning on another book. Nothing turns a book once it falls. Each book starts front cover up, tumbled up to about 46 degrees, and spins more about the vertical than end over end. Air drag slows a falling book until it first touches something (`PileLayout.airDrag`), so a drop's first wave, which starts out of view, high above the table, still lands at about 4 m/s at the scene's scale, about as fast as from 0.9 to 2 m up without drag; without it, about twice as many land face down. Without **Drop by month**, a drop's books fall at once: the scene builds every book first, then drops them in waves of 16 that each start a frame after and 13.2 units above the wave before, so the waves never meet in the air (`PileLayout.waveRise`), and each later wave lands faster, up to 5.5 m/s, the fastest that drag lets a book fall. Books that a library update adds to a drop start at the bottom again once the last wave's highest book has fallen below them, however many waves started under it, and 0.95 seconds after the last wave at soonest (`PileLayout.waveDelay(from:to:)`), so they fall clear of the books above them. Its center of mass sits a quarter of its thickness behind its middle, toward the back cover, so a book that lands on an edge or leans tips onto its back more often than onto its front. Books of a drop never start inside each other: a book that would reach into a lower one starts a little higher. Share images and videos lay every book front cover up instead (see [Share the desk](#share-the-desk)). To measure it, see [Check how books land](#check-how-books-land).
- Books knock as they land: one low knock, synthesized in eight versions a few percent apart in pitch and length, which hits take in a shuffled order that never repeats a version twice in a row, so a run of hits does not sound mechanical. A knock plays louder for a harder hit and is panned toward where it lands on screen. A burst of hits plays a sound at most every 30 milliseconds, except for a much harder hit, and the mix keeps headroom so a burst does not clip. The sounds are synthesized on the device, play off the main thread, mix with other audio, and follow the silent switch. Turn **Sounds** off in **Desk options** to silence them. On an iPhone, hits also play haptic taps, at most one every 60 milliseconds except for a much harder hit, whether or not **Sounds** is on. Taps play at least a third of full strength, so every tap is felt, and the haptic engine, which stops itself while nothing taps, starts again at the next tap. Haptics follow the hardware's support; the sounds follow the silent switch.
- Touch and hold a book for a quarter second to pick it up: it lifts 8 cm off what it rests on, and on an iPhone a light haptic tap plays. Drag to carry it, and let go to throw it (`PileCarry`). Tap a book, lifting your finger sooner, for its details (see [Details from the Desk scene](#details-from-the-desk-scene)); a finger that moves more than 10 points first does neither. Two fingers move the camera as they would a map (`PilePinch`): the table under them follows them as they drag, spreading them zooms toward them, and turning them turns the table with them. The camera slides only as far as the zoom leaves room for, so at the first view, which shows the whole table, two fingers turn and zoom but do not slide it. On the Mac, a drag past 10 points picks the book up instead, and the wheel zooms and turns (see [On the Mac](#on-the-mac)). **Drop again** starts a new drop and redraws books whose design or font changed since the last one.
- A held book follows your finger with a slight lag, 8 cm over whatever lies under it, rising over taller books on the way, and the walls keep it between the bars. A book picked up tilted eases flat over about half a second, front or back cover up, whichever is nearer; books that leaned on it lose their support and tip over. It leans into a change of pace, leading edge down, up to 20 degrees, swings back past level, and settles, as if it hung from your fingertip (`PileSwing`). It stays a physics body, so it shoves the books it meets instead of passing through them, and their hits knock and tap. Books that lay on it ride along until they slide off. Let go, and it flies at your finger's speed, at most 3 m/s at the scene's scale, tossed up by a fifth of that speed and tumbling forward, then lands, tumbles, and knocks into the books it hits. A finger that stops before letting go sets the book down. While you hold a book, two fingers do not move the camera, and a second finger that lands before a hold picks a book up cancels the hold and the tap.
- **Tilt** is hidden for now: **Desk options** does not offer it, and the pile treats any saved choice as **Off**, so it reads no motion and shakes do nothing (`PileTilt.isOffered`). Its code remains. While it is offered, on an iPhone that reports motion, **Tilt** in **Desk options** sets how the pile follows the phone, and the app saves the choice:
  - **Off** (default) keeps the table level and stops reading motion, so shakes do nothing either. Throwing books by hand is the scene's main play, and few people tilt their phone much while they hold it.
  - **Tip the table** tips it. The pile responds to the change from how you held the phone when tilting began, up to about 27 degrees, and books slide toward the edge of the screen you tip down, however you have turned the view.
  - **Phone is the box** makes the pile a closed box fixed to the phone. The table is its floor, the screen's glass is its lid, and its sides run from the camera along the screen's sides, the navigation bar's lower edge, and the tab bar's edge, so books inside it stay in view. Gravity follows the phone at full strength, as if the camera were fixed to it: held about 28 degrees up from flat, the camera's angle, the table is level; upright, the books fall to the bottom of the screen; face down, they fall onto the glass and lie flat against it. The box fits the view as it first shows, so in this mode two fingers zoom but do not turn the view, and a turned view eases back. The box closes once every dropped book has landed, and no sooner than 2.5 seconds after the last books drop (`PileLayout.boxDelay`), and opens while books drop, since they start above it, and while a book flies to or from its details; while it is open, the table stays level. A book past the glass or the sides as the box closes, such as one high against the far wall, behind the navigation bar, moves just inside them; books lying on the table stay where they are. The glass stays where the screen is at the default zoom, so zoomed in, books on it come close to the camera.
- With **Tilt** set to **Tip the table** or **Phone is the box**, a hard shake, about 1.8 g, throws the books against the jolt, hopping and tumbling, at most every 0.3 seconds. Their hits knock and tap as other hits do. A held book, a book flying to or from its details, and the stacks of **Reduce Motion** stay put. In these modes, the pile reads motion 60 times a second while it shows.
- With **Reduce Motion** off, **Drop by month** in **Desk options** drops a year's books a month at a time, in finish order, January first, and the last 12 months' from the month they start in, which also comes last when books were finished in it this year. A month's books drop week by week (days 1–7, 8–14, and so on), and **All Time** year by year, with unfinished books last; the menu names the choice for the time frame, such as **Drop by week**. The app saves it, and changing it drops the books again. Periods without books are skipped, and each period starts at least 1.4 seconds after the last, once the last period's highest book has fallen below its books. As a period's first books fall, its name, such as "January", "Aug 8–14", or "2023", glows on the table in front of the pile for 1.6 seconds, drawn over the books in near-white letters with a thin black outline and a soft dark halo, so the pile cannot hide it and it reads on pale covers (a book lifting toward its details ends the glow). The name is about two thirds of an iPhone's width. It keeps that size in views up to 820 points on their shorter side, the height of the Mac's default window, and grows past that, up to twice as large: 1.75 times full screen on a 14-inch MacBook Pro and twice on larger displays (`PileCalendar.scale(in:)`). The lamp keeps its usual warm white throughout. With the drop by period on, books a library update adds drop a new pile instead of joining the books still waiting.
- With **Reduce Motion** on, the books appear in tidy stacks, spines toward you, each book shifted a little and turned up to about 4 degrees so its spine faces a little left or right, once the spinner has shown while they get ready, and tilt, shaking, and the drop by period are off; **Desk options** hides the drop by period, and **Tilt** while it is offered. The stacks hold still: picking up a book frees only that book and the books resting on it. A held book neither swings nor flies: letting go drops it where it is. **Stack again** replaces **Drop again**. Tapping a book pushes its details, as **Stack** and **Shelf** do, and the pile is as you left it when you come back.
- Invisible walls keep the books between the navigation bar and the tab bar, and on the Mac clear of the sidebar, sized to the pile the drop plans (`PileTray.pileHeight(of:over:)`): the far and side walls stand where the top and sides of the view meet a pile as high as the books' volume spread over the table, doubled for a loose pile, plus 10 cm for books lying across others, up to 45 cm for a full drop, and the near wall stands where the bottom of the view meets the table. A year's pile therefore reaches close to the navigation bar, and a full drop, whose books stand on end against the walls, keeps room for them. With **Tilt** set to **Tip the table** when a drop starts, the walls keep a full drop's room, since tipping slides the whole pile against one wall; choosing it later keeps the walls until the next drop. The walls reach a ceiling above where the books start, which rises with the view's width, as in a wide Mac window, and 13.2 units for each wave of a drop past the first (`PileLayout.ceiling(over:waves:)`). The walls and the ceiling stand 1.2 m thick, and the walls reach below the table (`PileLayout.wallThickness`): a book struck in a falling pile can leave it at up to about 25 m a second, too fast for continuous collision detection to stop reliably, and physics then pushes it back out of the wall's inner face. The walls and the lamp's pool follow the view's size until the first books drop, since a tab's first layouts pass through sizes it never shows, and each new pile fits them to its books; books a library update adds keep the walls, though the ceiling rises when they add a wave. A sidebar over one side moves the walls' middle off it: the side walls follow the view's two edges at the near wall and stay inside them beyond, where perspective widens the view. **Dominoes** hands the books to the dominoes as they lie (see [Dominoes](#dominoes)); switching tabs keeps the pile as you left it (see [Desk](#desk)).
- **Back to desk** hands the dominoes' books back wherever they lie, standing, fallen, or gathered, without a new drop: they stay there as rigid bodies to pick up and throw, or to knock over, and keep moving as they moved. Walls go up along what the view shows between the bars, turned with the view when the dominoes' camera had turned it, and around any book outside it on the table, and the camera keeps the view it had, which stays the view it zooms out to and the view a lifted book's flight returns to, until the next drop. The box of **Phone is the box** stays open meanwhile, since it fits only the default view. **Drop again** drops a new pile.
- A drop builds every book before its first wave falls, a wave and two books at a time: each wave waits for its own fonts and covers while the next wave's covers download, all the waves within 1.5 seconds together and each at least a tenth of a second, and a font or cover that comes later reaches its book when it arrives. Period by period, and for the stacks of **Reduce Motion**, each wave waits at most 1.5 seconds for its own fonts and covers, and the next wave's covers download while it falls. The first wave also waits for the room's light, so the books enter lit. A book whose cover arrives later falls with its title on its cloth color, and the cover replaces the title when it arrives. A book whose font arrives later is lettered in its design's system typeface, so a connection that stalls never holds the pile back; the font goes on loading, and once the wave's fonts have loaded, the book is drawn again in its own, one book at a time, where it lies or before it falls (`BookFactory.prepare`). The dominoes' books wait for their fonts and covers the same way, 1.5 seconds for each batch. Textures stay small and grow with a book's length up to 24 cm, so books up to that length are about as sharp: a book at least 24 cm long has a cover of at most 224 pixels and a spine 384 pixels long. The 300-book maximum needs an estimated 70 MB of GPU memory for a typical library's sizes, and at most 147 MB. Books of one shape share a mesh, a new pile keeps only the meshes its books use, and books share page, cloth, and ribbon materials by color. The room's light is built once and shared with share images.

VoiceOver reads the scene as one element, such as "Desk with 39 books", with its time frame and a **Drop again** action; open **Library** to go through books one at a time. The **Setting up** spinner is a separate element; how VoiceOver reads it, and how it shows before the stacks of **Reduce Motion**, have not been checked. While a book's details show over the pile, VoiceOver reads the details and **Back** instead, and the escape gesture goes back. The **Setting up** spinner, the books' entry from above the view, the lamp's shadows, the dust, the power saving under heat and Low Power Mode, the pause and wake, frame rate and rendering cost, the details flight and throw, how closely the book follows the details as they scroll, picking up, carrying, and throwing a book, the sounds, and the stability of tall stacks have been checked only in the Simulator; check them on a device. The Simulator plays no haptics, so feel the pickup tap and how a carried book trails and swings under a real finger on a device. It also draws no soft shadows under the books, with or without the power saving. The lamp's shadows have hard edges on devices too: the lamp keeps RealityKit's default shadow quality, at which its light size has no effect. The Simulator reports no motion, so check **Tilt**, **Phone is the box**, and shakes on a device, including whether walking or tapping reaches the shake threshold and what reading motion costs; Debug builds can fake gravity and a shake (see [Preview with a local library](#preview-with-a-local-library)).

#### Details from the Desk scene

With Reduce Motion off, tapping a book lifts it out of the pile and into its details, the same RealityKit model throughout, as the Apple TV's Book Wall does. If the pile cannot lift the book, the details push instead.

- When books rest on the book and it has a clear way out along its length or width, it first slides out from under them (`BookExit`, as for [Dominoes](#dominoes)), moved by its pose so it drags none of them, and they fall into its place. It then rises straight up, 0.15 units past the top of the books that lay over it, pushing any book still on it up or aside, then flies on a spring to where the details show their book: centered in the same 460-point stage, facing you in the same three-quarter pose, with its spine showing. A book more than half a unit above that stack, such as one still falling, does not count. The rise takes 0.14 to 0.3 seconds, less for a lower rise, so a book with nothing on it leaves at once. The flight takes about a second and overshoots slightly. At the top of the rise, the book stops colliding, and the books it carried, which rode up on it, fall into its place under gravity and settle there, while the rest of the pile keeps moving. As the book flies on, it passes through a book that rode up on it for a frame or two. Every book over or touching the lifted book wakes as it starts to slide or rise, stays awake through the slide, and wakes again as the book stops colliding, so no book that had come to rest on it stays hanging in the air.
- A walnut veil, the color of the details' backdrop, darkens the pile behind the book as it flies until the pile no longer shows, and the camera eases back to its default zoom and position, keeping its turn. Clear of the pile, the book is drawn as the pushed details draw theirs, without the pile's lights or its ribbon, and with each face shaded as the details shade it. The veil carries the pushed details' lamp glow, centered behind the book where the details' stage stands, tinting the walnut as the pushed details tint it, so a veil that is still fading lets the pile show through evenly. About a second after the tap, the details fade in over the veil. They are the same details that **Stack** and **Shelf** open, with **Change cover**, the spine design, and **Share**. The book scrolls with them, a drag turns it as it turns the pushed details' book, and **Change cover** retextures it. Its cover and spine draw at the details' sharpness while they show, then return to the pile's smaller textures when the book lands back in the pile.
- Tap the bare desk twice within 0.35 seconds, or double-click it on the Mac, to gather the books: for 2.5 seconds every loose book slides along the desk toward the middle of where the books lie, pulled harder the farther out it is, and the pull rises and dies away, so a spread-out pile clumps together without being sorted or stacked (`BookPileScene.gatherBooks()`). Reduce Motion's still stacks stay put. VoiceOver offers it as **Gather books**.
- **Arrange** in the bar holds **Drop again**, one menu so the iPhone's bar keeps room for the time frame button, and flies every book on the desk into a **Cube**, a **Pyramid**, or a **Wall** (`BookPileScene.arrange(_:)`), centered between the walls. Every book lies flat, front cover up with its spine toward you, in courses of rows side by side, thickest books first, so each course's books are about as thick as one another; each rests on the highest book under it, shifted up to 3 mm and turned up to 2 degrees, with 1.5 cm between neighbors (`PileShape`). The cube is the grid of stacks nearest a cube; the pyramid's tiers each lose a book and a row and rise at about 48 degrees, its base giving up the courses the top doesn't need; the wall is one book deep where its height allows, about 2.5 times as long as it is high, each course shifted by half a book. Shapes stay under 12 units high where the tray allows (`PileShape.tallest`), and a wall one book deep under 18. The books fly as the dominoes' books gather into their spread (`DominoFinale.Gather`, with no book held back for a flourish): they rise into a messy cloud, then land one by one, bottom course first, with a soft knock, along ways planned so no book passes through another, all within 2.6 seconds where speeding up allows. As the books rise, the camera eases to look across the shape, 40 degrees down instead of the pile's 62 (`PileShape.viewPitch`), at its middle, keeping your turn, and zooms as close as it can while the whole shape shows clear of the bars and the Mac's sidebar, out past the pile's farthest view if a tall shape on a narrow view needs it, so the shape fills the view and its height shows; a pinch or turn takes over, and the next drop returns the camera to its default view. With **Reduce Motion** on, the camera moves there at once. Then the shape stands still, as kinematic bodies, so tall shapes don't slip apart as the simulation settles them: picking up a book frees it and the books resting on it, and a book that strikes the shape faster than 15 units a second (`PileShape.strike`), half a hard throw, turns every book back into a rigid body, so the hit knocks the shape down. While they fly, taps, picking up, **Gather books**, and **Dominoes** do nothing, and **Arrange**'s shapes are unavailable, as they are while books drop; **Drop again** stays available. With **Reduce Motion** on, the still stacks move straight into the shape and stay still.
- While the details show, the pile's gestures, tilt, shaking, **Arrange**, **Dominoes**, **Share**, **Desk options**, and the time frame button give way to **Back** and **Make share image**; on the Mac, Escape and **Go > Back** (Command-[) also go back, and the window takes the book's title and author. Tapping another book does nothing until the book in flight is back. The book's **Share** opens the Share tab and keeps the details and the pile, as pushed details do, so they are as you left them when you return to **Desk**.
- **Back** fades the details out in 0.2 seconds, returns the pile's controls, and throws the book back where it lay. It waits out any pause from the changing controls, then lands about a quarter second later, tumbling, and crashes into the pile there, scattering the books it hits, while the veil lifts and the camera returns. A throw from farther away takes longer, so the book flies no faster than 80 units a second on average. Its first hit keeps a quarter of its sideways speed and half of its tumble, so it stays near where it lands. The details pose can lie outside the walls, so the book passes through them until that hit. The hit plays a hard knock and, on an iPhone, a strong haptic tap.
- Switching tabs other than through **Make share image**, a new pile (for example, after a library change removes the book), or a share image that starts rendering closes the details at once and drops the book back over its place without the throw. Each also ends a throw in progress the same way. A task from an earlier lift or return cannot reopen or move anything.

[Dominoes](#dominoes) lift a book to its details the same way, with the same code (`BookLifter` and `LiftedDetails`), but set it back down where it lay rather than throw it.

#### Share the desk

**Share** in Desk's bar, while the Desk scene shows, opens **Share desk**. It spreads the pile's books front cover up across the table and renders a 1080-pixel-wide PNG in Story, Portrait, or Square format. **Shuffle** arranges them anew. A new library, or a new design for a book the image shows, such as one the Apple TV uploads or a generation run saves while the sheet is open, draws a new image once the image in progress is ready; after a new design alone, the shown image stays shareable meanwhile. The share button waits for the current image and reports the result as the Share tab does (see [Share](#share)).

- The heading names the time frame, such as "2025" or "November 2025", in New York. For **All Time**, it shows the years the books were finished, such as "2014–2026". Below it is the book count. Like the Share tab's **My reading** images, the image letters your profile link in its bottom margin once the app has read your profile, and the preview shows it; **Show profile link**, under **Shuffle** and **Video**, leaves it out of the image and the share text. The shared image carries the same credit at its bottom edge as the Share tab's images; the preview leaves it out.
- Up to 150 books show, the first in the pile's order. Every book lies front cover up with its head away from you, turned up to about 10 degrees, in loose rows across the table between the heading and the bottom margin (`PileSpread`). Books in a row lie side by side without overlapping, and rows overlap a little at the books' ends. Rows nearer the camera hold fewer books, since perspective narrows the table toward you. The layout tries each number of rows and keeps the one whose books show largest once framed, so the books reach across the frame or down it, from a month of one book to 150 books. Fewer books are framed closer, with sharper textures. When every row holds one book, the books zigzag down the frame.
- Every other row goes down first and takes the thinner books, and the books of the rows between rest across their ends. Each book lies flat on the table or rests on the books under it, tilted as they hold it: its underside lies as low as it can while it stays on the table and on those books, so it touches what holds it and never floats or sinks into another book (`PileSpread.rest(_:)`). Books thicker than 4 cm, about 600 pages, go in the upper rows while thinner books remain, so a book that rests along one side of another tilts a few degrees, and about 15 at most. No physics runs.
- At least four fifths of every front cover shows. The layout measures, from the camera, how much of each front board, from where the spine starts to curve to the fore edge, no other book hides and the pile rect holds, below the heading's fade and above the bottom margin (`PileSpread.visibility(of:camera:in:of:samples:)`). It moves apart the rows, or the books of a row, that hide too much of a cover until every cover shows at least 83 percent. If the rows had to move apart, it lays the books out again with rows that far apart from the start, so the frame's shape still decides how many rows there are. The camera then fits every book in the pile rect.
- The seed decides which books share a row, their order, shifts, and turns, and which rows go down first, so **Shuffle** gives a different arrangement each time, and the same seed gives the same image.
- The image has the live pile's lamp, room light, and shadows, without dust. The lamp's pool covers the books.
- While the sheet is open, the live pile leaves the screen and keeps its books. Closing the sheet shows the pile as you left it.
- `ImageRenderer` cannot capture a RealityKit view, so `PileSnapshotRenderer` builds a `BookPileScene` that no view shows. It places the books as static bodies, renders one frame with RealityKit's `RealityRenderer` into a Metal texture, and copies the frame to memory. If that frame is one flat color, the sheet shows "Couldn't draw the desk" instead of sharing it. The renderer and its textures are released after each image.
- The image waits for its books' fonts, however long they take, and covers get up to 10 seconds to load. A book whose cover comes later shows its title on the cloth, and the image draws once more when its cover arrives, in the sheet and in the Share tab's Desk template alike, unless a newer image replaces it first. The layout runs away from the main actor while the sheet shows progress. `PileSnapshotRenderer.render(books:title:aspect:seed:username:model:)` is the entry point; the Share tab's [Desk template](#share) uses it too.
- One pile image or [video](#desk-video) renders at a time, in the order they start, and a render waits for one that is still finishing after its task was cancelled. `RealityRenderer` steps the same RealityKit engine that draws the live pile, and RealityKit's render thread can crash drawing the pile's meshes meanwhile. While any pile image renders, from the sheet or the Share tab, the live pile's table and books therefore leave its scene, and the renderer starts 100 milliseconds later, so the view can react first. The scene puts them back when the render ends. A Desk tab that is hidden has no `ARView` (see [Desk](#desk)), and its scene returns without the table and books if a render is still running when the tab shows again.

Offscreen rendering and the image itself have been checked only in the Simulator. Check them on a device. To measure how much of each cover an image shows, see [Check a share pile's covers](#check-a-share-piles-covers).

#### Desk video

**Video**, under the size picker in **Share desk** and in the Desk template's options on the Share tab, opens **Desk video**: in **Share desk**, it pushes the video within the same sheet, which goes back to the image, so only one sheet ever shows; on the Share tab, it opens a sheet. On a Mac, both sheets take the page size (`presentationSizing(.page)`), which leaves the preview room. **Desk video** films the same books falling into place on the lamp-lit table: a six-second movie at the chosen size, 1080 pixels wide, with the knocks of their landings, in the [video format](#video-format) that every video from the app uses.

- The books, heading, credit, table, lamp, and camera are those of the image, and its seed decides the arrangement. Each book falls onto its place in the image's spread, so the clip's last frame, which the Live Photo's still shows, matches the image: every book front cover up, with at least four fifths of its cover showing.
- Each book starts straight above its place, high enough that the heading hides it (`PileMovieTiming.startHeight(of:camera:headingY:aspect:)`), so it falls into view rather than appearing in the air. When the camera is so close that no height below it hides a book, the book falls in from the top of the frame instead. A book falls under the pile's gravity and the video's own air drag (`PileMovieTiming.airDrag`), so from 15 units up it lands at about the speed of a 7-unit drop without drag. It turns a little about the vertical and a level axis as it falls, and stops in its place.
- The books land in the order they go down, so a book lands only after the books it rests on: 0.4 seconds apart, or closer for a big pile, so that the last lands by 4.5 seconds and the last 1.5 seconds stay still (`PileMovieTiming.landings(_:)`). The first lands at 1 second at the earliest, so the clip opens on the empty table. A book shows only once its fall begins, so books waiting behind the heading cast no shadows. The camera holds the image's framing throughout.
- The books are kinematic: the renderer moves them along their falls, so no collision reports a landing, and the scene plays each landing's knock itself, as hard as the book lands.
- `PileSnapshotRenderer.renderMovie(books:title:aspect:seed:model:progress:preview:)` builds a `BookPileScene` that no view shows, as for an image. Each frame advances the scene by two 1/60-second steps and renders it into a Metal texture. `FrameComposer` draws it, with the heading and credit, into a pixel buffer from the encoder's pool on the GPU, and `PileMovieWriter` encodes it. The scene steps on the main actor; each frame waits for the GPU and the encoder without holding it, and is offered to the encoder with `appendImmediately` every 4 milliseconds until the encoder takes it, so at most one frame waits for it.
- Every wait of a frame, for the GPU and for the encoder, ends 10 seconds after the frame starts, and a missed deadline fails the video, so a lost frame or a stuck encoder cannot hold up the live pile and other share images. Finishing the frames and writing the movie must end within 30 seconds (`PileMovieWriter.finishTimeout`); those waits have no timeout of their own and do not return when cancelled, so the writer stops waiting at the deadline instead. The render's turn ends with the last frame: the live pile gets its scene back while the writer finishes the movie and the still.
- `ImpactFeedback` records the hits whose sounds would play, whether or not **Sounds** is on. The soundtrack is mixed from them (`ImpactMix`) once the frames are done: each hit's synthesized knock, at its volume and stereo position, starts with the first frame that shows the hit.
- The sheet shows the progress over every sixth frame, at half size, as it renders, then loops the finished clip, except with **Reduce Motion** on, when it waits for **Play**. A tap on the clip pauses it, and on an iPhone shows **Play** over it. The preview leaves out the credit, which the shared MP4 and the Live Photo carry: it shows the clip 40 pixels shorter at the bottom, where the credit sits (`ShareCredit.height`), and as much at the top, above the heading, filling the preview's width. It has no system playback controls, whose full-screen view would show the whole frame. If the render fails, the sheet shows "Couldn't film the desk". **Cancel**, going back, or closing the sheet stops the render. The screen stays awake while the video renders. When the app stops being active, such as when you switch apps, the render stops, since iOS suspends the GPU and the encoder in the background. It starts again when the app is active again, and the seed makes the same clip. The movie and its still stay in a folder in the temporary directory until the sheet closes; the first video of a launch deletes any that earlier launches left there when they ended with the sheet open.
- **Share** opens the share sheet with the MP4, which reports the result in a toast (see [Share](#share)). **Save as Live Photo**, a separate choice, asks to add to Photos, with the same permission that saving share images uses, then saves the clip's last frame as a JPEG paired with a QuickTime copy of the clip; on an iPhone, a toast reports the result. Once saved, the button reads **Saved to Photos** and dims, so the clip isn't saved twice.
- On a Mac, a row under the clip holds **Play** or **Pause** (Space), **Save as Live Photo**, **Share**, and **Save Video…** (Return), since a Mac sheet doesn't show a pushed view's toolbar items. A Mac sheet draws no back button either, so in **Share desk** the row starts with **Back** (Escape). It shows from the moment the video opens, so you can leave a render or a failed video. **Save Video…** saves the MP4, named like "Desk 2025.mp4", where you choose. A failure, including a failed share, or denied access to Photos, which names **System Settings > Privacy & Security > Photos**, shows in an alert in the sheet, since the main window's toasts sit behind it. **Share desk** reports a failure to prepare or share its image the same way. The JPEG and the copy carry the same content identifier, and the copy, which has a timed metadata track that the MP4 leaves out, marks its last frame as the still.
- The video takes its turn with pile images, from either tab: one pile renders at a time, in the order they start, and the live pile leaves its scene while any of them renders.

Rendering, frames, sound, the format, and the Live Photo metadata have been checked only in the Simulator; check render time, memory, and the look on a device. **Save as Live Photo** and posting to Instagram have not been checked. To check a video, see [Check a desk video](#check-a-desk-video).

#### Tall Stack

The Share tab's **Tall Stack** template stacks the time frame's finished books into one tower, first finished at the bottom, and states its real height. It renders offscreen in RealityKit as the Desk image does (`TowerSnapshotRenderer`, `TowerStage`), with the same hardcover books, spine designs, page edges, and desk, and takes turns with Desk images and videos. SwiftUI draws the render over the image's share-theme backdrop, with the headline, the profile link, and the credit as on other **My reading** images. The iPhone and the Mac draw the same image; the Apple TV has no Share. Its lamp is more neutral than the Desk's, about 3,600 K rather than 2,700 K (`TowerStage.lampColor`), on purpose, so the spines' colors read truer.

- `BookTower` builds the stack apart from any view, as plain data, and measures it. Each book lies flat, front cover up, with its spine toward the camera, on the book below it, shifted up to 1.2 cm and turned up to about 4 degrees about the vertical axis, by an amount the seed and the book's ID decide. Thickness comes from the book's page count (`BookShape`), so the tower's height is the sum of its books' thicknesses.
- The headline gives the height in the reader's units (`BookTower.heightText(_:locale:)`): feet and inches where the locale uses US units, such as "2 ft 11 in", otherwise centimeters under a meter and meters with two decimals, or one from 10 meters, such as "1.26 m". The height never breaks across lines.
- The tower stacks at most 150 books (`BookTower.maximumBooks`). A larger frame stacks the 150 books the most Hardcover readers rated, in the order you finished them, since popular books look best and readers recognize them; the headline and the count still measure every book in the frame. At 150 books, a Story's spines read as colored bands, and the loupe carries the titles.
- A low camera looks up the tower from a fixed eye height (`TowerCamera`). It frames the tower, the tape, and the worm on the left of the area under the headline, standing on its bottom edge, and beside them a loupe magnifies the newest four books so their titles read at phone size, with a line to the tower's top. A tower of 16 books or fewer has no loupe and takes the full width, since its spines already read.
- A tape measure hangs plumb beside the tower, clear of every book's shift, from a brass hook over the top book's end to its case on the desk, and a bookworm sits coiled at the tower's base on the right (`TowerWorm`): the icon's and the promo video's worm, with its back curled flat around its base and its front rising to its face. Both grow with a tower over a meter tall, up to three times, so they stay visible.
- A figure of known real height stands to the right of the tower at true scale, slightly behind it, and the line under the headline compares the tower with it in place of the book and page counts (`TowerFigure`). The figures, shortest first: a cat (25 cm), you, a phone booth (2.4 m), an elephant (3.3 m), a giraffe (5.5 m), a house (8 m), a windmill (12 m), a castle tower (20 m, Quaternius's stone tower), and a lighthouse (30 m). The tower gets the figure closest to its height, by ratio, counting a figure shorter than the tower twice as far as a taller one, so the tower tends to stand beside something it is growing into. The line reads "Taller than a windmill" from 5 percent over the figure's height, "As tall as a windmill" within 5 percent, and "Almost as tall as a windmill" below. You are a wooden artist's mannequin at your height, with your Hardcover profile photo on a flat round card held in front of its face like a mask, larger than the head; without a photo, the face stays plain wood. The line names you as "you": "Taller than you", "As tall as you", and for a shorter tower the highest landmark it passes, such as "Up to your waist": shins, knees, thighs, waist, chest, shoulders, chin, and eyes, at 15 to 93 percent of your height. **Your height** in the template's options sets the height, in whole centimeters, or in inches where the region measures in feet and inches, from 90 cm to 2.3 m (`ReaderHeightPicker`). It stays on this device, in user defaults (`ReaderHeight`), and doesn't sync through iCloud. Until you set it, the figure stands at 1.7 m, about an adult's average. A tower under 15 cm, 60 percent of the cat, has no figure and keeps the counts. When the tower stacks only its most popular 150 books, the figure shrinks with the drawn tower, so the two keep their real proportion. The lamp's pool widens to light the figure too, and the still's camera frames it with the tower; during the clip's fall the camera frames only the books, the tape, and the worm, so the falling books stay large, and the rewind returns to the still's frame. The models ship in `Bookworms/Figures` (`figure-<name>.usdz`).
- **Video** films a loop of about 2.9 seconds (`TowerSnapshotRenderer.renderMovie`, `TowerMovieTiming`): the still holds, the tower is pushed to tip back and toward the tape while the tape zips into its case and the loupe fades, the books fall under the physics engine and knock as they land, and the clip rewinds to the still. The fall is simulated before any frame renders, from the same poses and pushes for the same books and seed, so the same clip comes out each time. Each frame's camera frames the books where they lie a few frames ahead, backs away at once and comes nearer slowly, and rises to look down on the fallen books. The files are those of a desk video: an MP4 and a Live Photo.

#### Video format

Every video the app shares, a desk video, a Tall Stack's topple, or a dominoes run, is an MP4 in the format that Photos, Messages, and social apps such as Instagram accept most widely (`PileMovieWriter`): H.264 High profile video, 8-bit 4:2:0, at 30 frames per second and about 10 Mbit/s, tagged Rec. 709, with an AAC-LC soundtrack at 48 kHz in stereo, and the movie's index at the front of the file, so apps can play and upload it as it arrives. It has no other tracks or metadata. A desk video lasts 6 seconds, and a dominoes run at most 33 seconds. A story-size video is 1080 × 1920.

- `FrameComposer` draws each frame with Core Image on the GPU, converting the 3D render's Display P3 colors to Rec. 709; the few colors of the lamp-lit scene outside Rec. 709 clip to it.
- The frames are encoded as they come into a file of their own, with one input. A writer with several inputs pairs their samples as it writes and stops taking frames while it waits for sound it has not been given, which, fed one frame's sound at a time, it did partway through long clips. The sound therefore joins the frames in a second pass once the frames are done: it copies the frames without decoding them and encodes the soundtrack, offering each track its next sample in turn, so no track waits on another.
- The knocks are synthesized at 48 kHz, the soundtrack's rate, so the mix needs no resampling.

### Dominoes

**Dominoes** turns the books on the desk into a chain. **Dominoes** in the bar stands them on end in the order you finished them, oldest first; a push topples them, and, once every book has fallen, they gather into a face-up spread under a heading, which **Share** shares as a video of the run you watched or as an image. **Back to desk**, in its place in the bar, returns to the Desk scene with the books where they lie (see [Desk scene](#desk-scene)). The dominoes share the Desk scene's time frame, and choosing another time frame while they show stands that period's books in a new chain.

- The books on the desk stand: up to 300, the first in **Sort by**'s order that fit under the Desk scene's maximum, which a typical library's books all do. They stand in finish order whatever **Sort by** says: books finished on the same day stand in a fixed order, so the order does not change the chain, and **Desk options** offers no **Sort by** for Dominoes. Under **All Time**, books without a finish date, such as current reads, stand last, and no cobweb hangs before them, because no gap between finishes leads to them.
- The path starts at the top left and winds across the table in rows 50 cm apart, joined by half turns. The rows are long enough for the path to take about the shape of the visible table, or of the video's frame in a wider view (see below), up to 7.2 m, and the path reaches at most about 7.2 m deep, which fits the 9-meter table: even 300 doorstoppers make a path only about 52 m long. Each book's front cover faces back along the path, so the book falls onto its back cover, front cover up.
- With enough books, about 60 of typical thickness (`DominoLayout.wormRows`, five rows of chain), the rows instead fill the silhouette of one of the worms in the app icon, head first: a semicircular head at the far side of the table, then a body that narrows a little toward the bottom, which the icon's book hides (`DominoLayout.wormRowLengths(for:)`). Each row runs between the outline's two sides, less the turns' bulge, so the turns reach the outline, and the shape emerges row by row as the chain stands up and falls; it reads from the Overview camera and from the Follow camera's high opening and closing views. The head's radius is fitted so the rows and their turns just hold the chain, which ends partway along the last row. The spacing, the turns, and the cobwebs are as on even rows, and the Follow camera times each swing by the rows either side of its turn (`DominoPath` takes a length per row). The chain draws no eyes or smile. Shorter chains wind in even rows.
- On the Mac, **Video** in the Desk's options menu chooses the frame the dominoes' video records (`DominoVideoFrame`): **Portrait**, a 9:16 story, the default, or **Window**, the window's own shape. The choice rides on the view's layout (`PileViewport.videoFrame`), so the plan, the cameras, the darkened sides, the heading, the video, and its image all follow it, and changing it sets the chain up again from where its books lie. With **Window**, the whole run plays in the whole view, as on an iPhone, and nothing darkens. The iPhone offers no choice, because its view already has a story's shape.
- In a view wider than 9:16 that records a story, such as a Mac's window on **Portrait**, the whole run plays in the 9:16 frame that the video records: the view's full height, centered on the part of the view that the sidebar leaves clear as far as the view allows (`DominoFinale.area(in:)`). The rows take the shape of that frame below the toolbar, both cameras frame the chain and follow its front there (`DominoFinale.clearArea(in:)`), and a setup from the desk moves the camera to show the books and their places there, so the view inside the frame and the video show the same run, every book's fall included. The table shows on either side, darkened while the dominoes show, apart from a book's details, from the setup through the finale; touches and scrolls over it still reach the chain. On an iPhone, the frame is the whole view.
- The books stand one continuous chain, evenly spaced whatever the days between their finishes, so one push topples every book. A toppling book pivots on its leading bottom edge, the back cover's, so its head reaches at most its own height, 17 to 28 cm, past that edge. That height is its reach. Each domino weighs by its height alone (`DominoLayout.mass(height:)`), so a thin book knocks over a much thicker one instead of bouncing off it.
  - On a row, the gap from one book's back cover to the next book's front cover is 30 percent of the first book's height (`DominoLayout.gap(height:)`). The falling book strikes the next one about 95 percent of its own height up, or at the next book's top edge when that book is shorter, so a short book strikes even a doorstopper more than half way up.
  - On the turns, books stand farther apart where needed to keep their inner corners 5 mm apart. Those gaps stay within 40 percent of the first book's height.
- The chain falls in slow motion, at 0.8 times the simulation's full speed (`DominoScene.slowMotion`), as film shot at a higher frame rate shows it, and speeds up the longer it falls: the simulation's clock runs faster the farther the front has come, smoothly toward real time, 1.25 times the slow motion, and never past it (`DominoLayout.speedUp(at:)`), so a year's chain of about 35 units ends about 1.1 times as fast as it started, and a chain of 150 units or more falls nearly at real time by then. The clock sets the speed of gravity, the push, and every collision together, so the books fall exactly as they would at full speed, only slower at first; the knocks and haptic taps play as the collisions happen, so they keep pace. While the books set themselves up from the desk, the simulation runs at full speed, so the books still lying settle as they would, and while a book is out at its details, it stands still (see [Details from the dominoes](#details-from-the-dominoes)).
- Where 30 days or more passed between two finishes (`DominoLayout.cobwebDays`), a cobweb hangs across the gap between the two books' top edges: radial threads from an off-center hub, a spiral with some chords broken, and bridge threads along the sides, sagging toward the middle. Longer gaps string denser webs, with more radials, more turns, a deeper sag, more broken chords, and stray threads, up to the densest at 240 days or more. Each web's irregularity and opacity are seeded by its two books, so a chain stands the same webs each time. The webs are built in code (`Cobweb`), share one translucent silk material that catches the lamp, and have no physics, so they never block or slow a book.
