RealityKit generates each web's meshes asynchronously before its books stand.
- Once the chain has started, a web tears when either of its books tilts past about 3 degrees, from the falling book's side to the far side. Each radial's share of the web stays attached at its outer end to the edge of the nearer book and moves with it, while its threads spring back and bunch, swing down to hang below that point, slip, and fade within half a second. With **Reduce Motion** on, the whole web fades where it hangs. **Set up again** hangs the webs anew, and **Back to desk** removes them.
- **Dominoes** sets the chain up from the books as they lie on the desk (`DominoSetup`). Books still sliding from a throw get up to a second to slow below 3 cm a second, from which the desk stops them within millimeters. The books, the camera, and the lamp's pool then move across the table by whole tiles of its wood, which repeats every 1.5 m (`DeskStage.tile`), so the move does not show, until each book's place lies at least 15 cm from every book that still lies on the desk when that book lands, and the books and their places fit together in the smallest view (`DominoSetup.offset(of:aspect:)`). A book off the table, 8 m or more from its middle or more than 20 cm below its top (`DominoSetup.isOffTable(_:)`), counts for none of this, and at its turn it drops from above the view straight onto its place, passing through anything on its way. The camera eases out to show both, twice as fast as it moves when you switch **Camera**, and the lamp's pool spreads over them in a second. The first book waits until the camera has come half the way, for at most half a second, so the places it hops to show. The chain's cobwebs build while the books move, 16 books at a time with their meshes generating at once, and each hangs unseen until the book behind it stands.
  - The books go one after another in finish order, oldest first, ever faster: 0.08 seconds apart on average, or closer in a long chain, so the last is due within 1.4 seconds of the first (`DominoSetup.step(count:)`). The first books come about 1.8 times as far apart as the average and the last about 0.6 times, three times as fast, speeding up steadily, so the longer the setup runs, the faster its books come (`DominoSetup.step(after:count:)`, `DominoLayout.entryShare(_:of:)`). A frame starts every book due in it, however short the steps, so a long chain keeps its pace at 60 frames a second. A blocked book's turn moves on to its next check, so when it goes, the books after it keep their pace from then and no backlog builds up. A frame moves the setup on by at most 1/20 second, so a stalled frame slows the books rather than skipping their moves.
  - Each book first rights itself where it lies: it turns up onto its tail edge, about that edge's lowest point, until it stands upright on its tail, as a book stands on a shelf, a quarter turn in 0.28 seconds, slowly enough that books lying on it slide off rather than fly. A fallen domino rises back the way it fell. The book then hops to its place on an arc that falls under 2.25 times the desk's gravity, about 1.25 g at the scene's scale, so the hop is brisk (`DominoSetup.hopGravity`), and takes at least 0.16 seconds, turning about the vertical only, so it stays upright in the air. It carries across while its underside clears the books it passes over by 2.5 cm (`DominoSetup.lift(from:to:size:over:)`), and drops the last fifth of the hop straight down onto its place, so it never sweeps through the books standing beside it. It lands with a soft knock, and the cobweb in front of it fades in over a quarter second, or as it hangs if it builds later. A landed book is held in its place each frame until the chain's physics starts, since RealityKit can write a kinematic book's pose from its last frame in the air back over the pose its landing sets, which would leave it hanging above its place and then drop it there at once as the setup ends.
  - A blocked book waits up to half a second for a clear way, however far the setup has run. Before a book goes, its whole way is checked against every book that moves or stands, every 1/60 second (`DominoSetup.Schedule`): its arc rises higher over a book it would cross standing, and where its hop would cross a book that still moves, it turns up onto its tail and stands there, for up to 0.4 seconds, until its way clears, so the books after it need not wait for it. A book whose turn up onto its tail would cross a moving book waits where it lies, and its way is checked again every 1/30 second. A book whose way stays blocked for half a second (`DominoSetup.longestHold`) goes regardless, however far the setup has run; such a book collides with nothing until it lands, so it can pass through another but never shoves it. Each book can wait that long, so a setup's time has no cap: in the worst case, with every book blocked for its whole wait, 300 books take just under three minutes. The check covers the books that still lie only for the height of the arc, so a book rising or hopping beside one can pass through it. A book turning up onto its tail moves by velocity, so the physics pushes aside the books lying around it; a hopping book collides with nothing. The books that still lie are rigid bodies, so they settle as the books under them leave. Moving books are kinematic, and every book stays kinematic until the last has landed and every cobweb hangs; then the chain's physics starts, the lamp's pool moves over the chain, the camera hands over to the camera mode, twice as fast as it moves when you switch **Camera**, and the chain waits for a push, which it takes at once, even while the camera still moves.
  - With **Reduce Motion** on, the books stand in their places at once, and the camera cuts to the chain.
  - **Set up again** sets the books up the same way from where they lie, fallen or gathered.
- A chain of books that do not lie on the desk, such as another time frame's chosen while the dominoes show, or one whose books a memory warning freed, stands up from above instead. While its first books get ready, the spinning book shows over the table, as in the Desk scene. Then the books drop into their places one after another down the path, oldest first: each appears just above the top of the view as it shows, wholly out of sight, and drops straight down onto its place in 0.35 seconds, speeding up as it falls. Books are built 16 at a time, each batch while the batch before it gets its cobwebs, and the covers of the next two batches download ahead, so the work stays off the frames that show the drops. A batch starts dropping once the batch after it is built. The drops speed up as they go on, as the books from the desk do: the first come about 1.8 times as far apart as the average and the last about 0.6 times, three times as fast (`DominoLayout.entryGap(after:of:step:)`). On average they come a little slower than the books have taken to build, however slowly that is, so they start slower than the books build, never run out of built books, and keep dropping without a pause while later ones build (`DominoLayout.entryStep(count:perBook:)`). A chain built in one batch, or whose books are built at once, drops them 50 milliseconds apart on average, or closer in a long chain, so every drop starts within 1.2 seconds of the first (`DominoLayout.entryStep(count:)`). A dropping book pushes nothing and stands at rest once it lands, so the chain stands aligned, and each cobweb fades in over a quarter second once the book behind it lands. The **Overview** camera holds still and the **Follow** camera holds its high opening view while the books drop. With **Reduce Motion** on, the books appear standing. Until the chain's last book is built, the books built so far are not the whole chain, so the scene hands none of them over (`DominoScene.handOff()`): **Set up again** starts the chain over from above, and **Back to desk** drops the books onto the desk again.
- While the chain waits for a push, which it does once every book stands, a pointing hand floats before the first book, as a finger about to poke its front cover: the macOS cursor's glove, drawn from the `hand.point.right.fill` symbol in cream with a dark outline and a shadow on a plane 1.25 units across that turns about the finger to face the camera, upright from either side, with a faint, soft glow behind its palm that pulses slightly. The glow fades out smoothly well inside its edge and sits back from the fingertip, so neither it nor the hand reaches the book, even as the hand pokes. It hovers as the details' book drifts (`Drift`), scaled to the scene. A tap on the hand, or within 22 points of its middle, starts the chain, and so does a tap up to 16 points past it that hits no book, so a hand drawn small, far off, is still easy to hit; the hand pokes forward as the book tips, then fades. With **Reduce Motion** on, the hand and its glow hold still, and it only fades. The first time a chain waits for its push, a tip points at the hand once the camera has come to rest, and goes at the push (see [Desk](#desk)).
- A book that falls before the first push, such as one that does not stay upright, starts the chain as a push does. The chain counts as stopped when, for a second, no book has started to fall and the book at the front has stopped tipping, so a slow first tip after a push does not count. That can happen where a book leans on the next one without knocking it over, or, if RealityKit cannot build a book's mesh, at the empty place that book leaves, which may be wider than the book before it can reach. The hand then returns, above and behind the first upright book among the leaning book and the two after it, pointing down at it, so each tap moves the chain on, and VoiceOver announces that the chain stopped.
- Tap a book, or touch and hold it, for its details, before the push, while the chain falls, or once the books lie gathered (see [Details from the dominoes](#details-from-the-dominoes)). Taps on books do nothing while the books set themselves up or drop into their places, and while they gather. The dominoes are arranged, so no book is picked up, carried, or thrown there; throwing stays with the Desk scene. A drag that goes on after a touch and hold has lifted a book leaves the camera where it was. **Set up again** sets every book up again.
- **Camera** in **Desk options** chooses how the camera films the chain, and the app saves the choice:
  - **Overview** frames every book, standing or fallen, between the navigation bar and the tab bar, or in a wider view within the video's frame, with a little room around the path, so a year's chain fills about two thirds of an iPhone's width. It looks from 20 degrees toward the lamp's side rather than straight across the rows (`DominoScene.overviewYaw`): every other row stands with its fore edges toward the camera, which from straight ahead show only page edges, so the turn shows those rows' back boards and the other rows' front covers beside their spines, lit, while every book stands upright. After a push, the camera eases in a little, at most 5 percent, and, where the path is deeper than the view, eases up or down the view with the falling books. It does not chase the front along the rows.
  - **Follow** films the chain as a camera operator would, with slow moves that ease in and out. It opens on a high view of the whole path from the side it films from, and once the books stand, it cranes down and pushes in toward the first book over four to nine seconds, longer when it has farther to turn or zoom, and holds low behind it. While the books fall, it films the front low, from about 8 to 25 degrees above the table, and far enough back that a typical book at the front fills about a fifth of the view's height, a little more in the tighter shot about one row in six gets, so several books either side of the front and some of the path ahead show. It always films from the side of the rows that have already fallen, so no standing row hides the front, and each row gets its own angle, height, and distance. It chases every row from behind and beside the front, so the books fall away from it rather than toward it, and the rows' alternating directions put it on the front's left along one row and on its right along the next. It films the rows where the standing books show their spines from nearer beside the front, and the rows where they show their fore edges from nearer behind. Around the half turn between two rows, it swings in an arc to the next row's angle, craning up and banking slightly as it goes. A bigger swing takes longer, at about 26 degrees a second on average over 1.2 to 2.6 seconds, and where the rows are long enough, every swing leaves at least half a second of each row steady. The swing starts before the front reaches the turn, so the camera leads the turn rather than lagging it. Wherever a row is short for the chain's pace, which grows as the chain falls, it films nearer beside the front, whichever side the books show, so every swing fits between the turns; where a row leaves less than 1.2 seconds for a swing, the swing takes only the time there is and turns less. Round about a fifth of the turns, it films the rows either side nearly beside the front and cranes high over the turn with a small swing. Once the camera lands on the first book, its height, bearing, and distance also drift slowly, as a drone's would. It pushes in and lowers its angle for a few of the densest cobwebs, over gaps of about 85 days or more and at most one every five or six seconds, and it films the row where one of them tears from as near behind the front as the swings either side allow, so the web comes toward the camera as it tears. It also pushes in when the chain slows or stops, and when the chain stops, it comes to rest just past the last book that fell rather than running on and backing up. These pushes do not add up: the strongest one sets how close the camera comes. Whatever the move, it keeps looking at the front: the point it looks at follows the front alone, and as the chain speeds up, its springs stiffen in proportion and its swings round the later turns are timed for the faster pace, so they turn less, and the front stays about as near the middle of the view as at the chain's first books. The camera follows the front to the last book and eases to a stop there, settling on where the book lands and looking down on the fallen books from about 21 degrees or more, as the finale takes the camera away. After a pinch or drag, the next push eases the camera back to the front over about two and a half seconds. `DominoCameraRig` computes each pose from the path, the front, and the time alone, and keeps the camera above the fallen books and clear of the standing ones. Critically damped springs smooth its motion, and limits on its speed, acceleration, turn rate, and how fast its distance changes back them up.
- Once every book has fallen, the finale gathers the books into the Share tab's Desk image layout (`PileSpread`): every book face up with its head away from the camera, in loose rows that show at least four fifths of each cover (see [Share the desk](#share-the-desk)), framed below the heading's room and above the tab bar. In a view wider than 9:16 that records a story, such as a Mac's window on **Portrait**, the spread fills the 9:16 frame that the video records instead of the whole view: the view's full height, centered on the part of the view the sidebar leaves clear as far as the view allows, with the books 16 points in from its sides and clear of the sidebar, 24 points above its bottom, and below a heading room of 28 percent of its width (`DominoFinale.area(in:)`, `DominoFinale.spreadRect(in:)`). The table shows on either side, darkened as it is through the whole run, so the window shows the frame that the video and its image share, and they show every gathered book. The spread is laid out as soon as every book stands, away from the main actor, for the view's size, and turned to the camera's yaw when the finale starts (`DominoFinale`):
  - The moment the last book tips, the camera, in either mode, starts one move from the view it films to the spread's view, where it comes to rest 2 seconds later. The spread lies over the part of the table the chain fell on, so the spread's view frames about what the overview did, and the books gather in view. It swings about the point it looks at, so it cranes up and pulls back in one sweep; it leaves with the speed and heading the camera had as the last book fell, which die away within about half a second (`DominoFinale.coast`), so it turns straight into the move rather than stopping dead or drifting on with the camera mode, and the move gathers speed over its first third and spends the rest slowing to a stop. For its first 0.6 seconds, after **Follow**, the point it swings about moves onto where the last book lands, so the camera pulls back from the last book, centering it, rather than turning away from it; after **Overview**, which shows the whole chain, that point stays where the middle of the view meets the table. It then moves to the point the spread's view swings about, where the middle of that view meets the table, and arrives as the camera rests. A camera you moved with a pinch or drag starts the move at rest. Pinches and drags wait until the last book has landed in the spread, so they never move the camera into or away from the book as it passes the camera; a new **Camera** choice applies from the next chain.
  - The fallen books lie still for a second after the last book falls, while the camera settles. Their ways into the spread are planned meanwhile, away from the main actor, once every book lies still, from 0.3 seconds after the last book falls and at most 0.9 seconds after, so that no book passes through another (`DominoFinale.Gather`). A frame moves the finale on by at most 1/20 second, as it does the setup, so a stalled frame slows the finale, as it slows the physics, rather than skipping its moves. Then every book rises off the table at once, over 0.7 seconds, into a messy cloud (`DominoFinale.Gather.cloud`): over the first half it slides out across the table, mostly across the chain's line, 1.3 to 2.4 units, into the gap between rows, climbing only as fast as the books it lay against, then climbs or sinks to a height of its own. About three in ten of the books that leave first hover just above the table, 0.1 to 0.45 units above where they lay, and the rest hover at random up to 2 units above a lane that every book can glide through clear of the spread's places, so a book that lay under another often hovers above it. Each turns at odd angles as it goes, up to about 52 degrees about the axis through its covers and 20 degrees about its other axes, and rises farther by as much as its turn lowers its bottom, so a book hovering high stays above the lane and one hovering low above the table. The books are placed one at a time in a shuffled order, each taking the first of several tries that hovers clear of the spread's last book across the table and whose way meets no other book's; a book that no try fits rises straight up, turned if it may, higher than the books it lay on, which keeps every book apart. Once risen, each book floats gently up and down, up to 6 millimeters every 1.6 seconds, each at its own beat. The books then leave in the spread's order, January's first: the first speeds up 0.2 seconds after the books have risen, and each next at least 0.06 seconds later, or sooner so that every book may leave within 0.8 seconds of the first, so the more books, the more move at once (`DominoFinale.firstLeave`, `leaveStep`, `leaveSpan`). Each book slides out across the table from over the books still hovering, by the shortest move that clears them, the most nearly toward its place of those as short. Where its way allows, it starts drifting slowly out as soon as the books have risen, gaining speed, so the cloud keeps moving; otherwise it waits and does not drift. Once it speeds up, it finishes sliding out over the first 30 percent of its flight, rising 3 centimeters, or level from under a book still hovering over it, sinks into the lane by 45 percent, carries across from 35 percent, tips from 40 percent to lie face up as its place does, bowing to one side by up to a tenth of the way, at most 1.5 units, and settles straight down onto its place for the last 15 percent with a soft knock. Each stretch eases in and out, so the books float rather than fly like thrown books. A flight from its speed-up takes from 0.75 to 1.05 seconds, longer the farther it carries, varied by up to 15 percent, and the books land at their own pace rather than together. In the lane a book passes over the books in their places and under the cloud. The books are planned one at a time, the book ready first whose place's books are planned first: each speeds up as soon as its way, checked every 1/120 second with room to spare in the air, meets none of the books planned before it or still hovering, and lands at least 0.1 seconds after the books its place rests on; while it would meet one, it does not drift, waits longer, flies a little longer or shorter, or glides higher. Every book but the last lands within 2.6 seconds of starting to rise; a gathering that would land later, also for the time its planning took past the pause, speeds up its flights, not the rise, by up to 2.2 times, which keeps every book's way and so keeps them apart.
  - The spread's last book is the last book read: the chain's last book with a finish date, or its last book in a chain without one. The spread puts it down after every other book (`PileSpread`'s `last`), so no book rests on it. It floats up with the others but never joins the cloud: the chain's last book, it lies under the books that fell onto it, so it waits while they rise, slides out from under them along the table, and rises beside the cloud, as high as the cloud's lowest books, out of every other book's way, where it floats while the rest land. 0.2 seconds before the last of them lands, so one move flows into the next, it flies along one curve that never stops: it zooms out toward the camera in 0.3 seconds, turning its cover to the viewer, then passes the camera in slow motion for 0.4 seconds, covering only the middle sixth of its way as it turns 10 degrees about its length, through the middle of the spread's area, the whole view on an iPhone and the video's frame on a Mac, its cover square to the viewer, head up, near enough to fill 45 percent of the area's width, or 42 percent of its height for a book too tall for that (`DominoFinale.passPose(size:seenBy:in:of:)`). Then it slams down into its place in 0.1 seconds, in a nearly straight line, speeding up hard all the way and lying flat for the last third of the drop, so it lands flat on the books under it, with a deep knock, the firmest haptic tap, and, while the lamp's dust shows, a soft haze of it blown out from under the book: about 40 puffs of the lamp's warm light for each unit of its edges, each a soft, slightly uneven glow, burst out at once from random places in a band along them just above its underside, so the haze has no outline, roll outward and up over the books around it, never down into their covers, slowing within about half a book's width, and grow by half as they thin out steadily, gone within 1.2 to 1.8 seconds. They brighten what lies behind them, as dust in a beam does, so they never hide a cover (`DeskStage.puff(under:size:)`). The puff's emitters are built for the last book once the chain stands, because RealityKit holds up the frames after it sets up a new particle emitter, which would freeze the slam's first frames (`DeskStage.preparePuff(size:)`). The camera jolts with the blow, without Reduce Motion: it shakes up and down about 18 times a second, at most 0.17 units up or down, 0.06 to the side, and 0.006 radians of roll, dying away within 0.45 seconds, before the books settle (`DominoFinale.jolt(_:time:)`). The slam's blow knocks the table down, and physics carries the spread's books from there: the table, a heavy slab a little hollow underneath, about 40 times as heavy as a typical book, gives a few millimeters and springs back 14 times a second, dying away within a tenth of a second (`DominoFinale.table(after:kick:)`), at most 10 units a second down. The table moves at that motion's own speed (`DominoFinale.tableSpeed(after:kick:)`), so its motion doesn't depend on how long a frame runs. The spread's books are rigid bodies until 1.2 seconds after the slam, so they leave the table as it drops, and it bounces them a few millimeters into the air as it springs back; they land on the table and each other, and may shift a little. Then they hold where they lie. Out of the lamp's beam, which a book flying toward a camera across the table from the lamp leaves, the room's light on it brightens by about the light it lost, up to 4.5 stops, so it stays lit and readable, a little darker than the books under it, rather than a dark slab (`DominoFinale.boost(at:over:radius:)`). From the moment the last book tips, the book that will pass the camera loads a cover of up to 768 pixels on its long side, about as many as its cover fills on an iPhone's screen as it passes and more than three times the chain's, so its lettering stays sharp (`DominoFinale.flybyCoverPixels`).
  - Once the shake ends, the heading fades in: the time frame and the books and their pages, such as "39 books · 18,683 pages", in the share images' type (`DominoFinaleCard`), scaled to the width of the widest 9:16 frame the spread's area holds (`DominoFinale.headingScale(in:)`) and centered over the part of that area the sidebar leaves clear, over the books (`DominoFinaleCard.insets(in:frame:)`), so on a Mac it shows where and as large as the video shows it, and a **Window** video shows it as large as a story of the window's height. The live heading has no credit; an exported image or video adds "Created with Bookworms for iPhone" at the very bottom of the frame.
  - The books stay dynamic for 1.2 seconds after the slam while they settle (`DominoFinale.settleTime`), then turn kinematic, so the spread holds. Taps and long presses on the books do nothing while the books gather; once they lie gathered, a book lifts to its details and **Back** sets it down in its place in the spread. **Set up again** sets the books up again from the spread.
  - With **Reduce Motion** on, the camera cuts to the spread's view and the books appear in their places a second after the last falls.
- **Share**, at the bar's trailing edge once the books have gathered, offers **Video** and **Image** of the run you watched in the share sheet, which reports the result in a toast (see [Share](#share)). The scene records the run as it plays (`DominoRecorder`), so nothing renders again.
  - The recording runs from the push through the finale until 2 seconds after the heading starts to fade in. The video then holds its last frame still for 3 seconds (`DominoMovieTiming.lastFrameHold`), so viewers see the gathered books under the heading before it ends: the writer repeats that frame 30 times a second, which renders nothing again, and the soundtrack runs on under it, silent once the last knock fades. A recording cut off at 30 seconds before the books gather ends without the hold. **Video** is a 1080 × 1920 MP4 (see [Video format](#video-format)) with the hits' knocks, and **Image** a PNG of its last frame; with **Window** on the Mac, both take the window's shape with its longer side 1,920 pixels, rounded to even pixels (`DominoRecorder.frameSize(for:)`), and show the whole view, and a window resized mid-run keeps that shape, framed at the new view's width or height. With **Portrait**, both show the part of the view that a 9:16 frame holds. On an iPhone, the frame spans the full width, placed so that the heading sits at a story's top margin, 250 pixels down, where the share images put theirs, unless that would leave out the bottom of the scene above the tab bar. A view wider than 9:16, such as a Mac's window, shows its full height in a frame centered on the part of the view that the sidebar leaves clear, where the whole run plays (`DominoFinale.area(in:)`), so the frame holds still from the push to the end and lines up with the darkened sides of the window. While the window keeps its size, the frame never moves or changes size, so the heading and the credit hold their places. A window resized mid-run moves the frame with the darkened sides from the next frame on; the heading keeps the place in the frame it had as the recording started, a little off the live one after a change of the window's height. The wall's gradient fills in around the scene, as it does on screen. The heading appears where the live view shows it and fades in as it does, and "Created with Bookworms for iPhone" sits at the bottom edge throughout, about 10 pixels up. The bars, with the time frame button, are not recorded.
  - The finale keeps live speed. When the run would make the recording longer than 30 seconds, before the 3-second hold, the recording keeps fewer of the view's frames while the books fall, so the run plays faster than live: it estimates the run's length from how long the chain has taken to fall as far as it has and how much faster the rest will fall as it speeds up (`DominoCameraRig.fallTime(to:)`), or, at first, from the path's length, and plays the rest of the run as fast as it must to leave room for the finale and the heading, easing between speeds (`DominoMovieTiming`). A year's chain plays at live speed. While a chain stops, its estimated length grows, so a long stop plays faster still.
  - RealityKit's `postProcess` render callback, on its render thread, passes each frame on to the screen with a blit, and, for each frame the recording keeps, `FrameComposer` draws it with the heading and credit into a pixel buffer that the hardware encoder takes once the GPU is done. The writer's input expects frames in real time (`expectsMediaDataInRealTime`). Frames the encoder has no room for wait for it in order, up to six; past that every other waiting frame is dropped, counting back from the newest, so the recording never holds up the view, and a stalled encoder leaves a few frames spread across the stall rather than one frame frozen through it. The video log's "Recorded N of M frames" counts the frames the encoder took of the video's M. The recording arms when the chain stands, so its start-up cost falls before the push: the writer is made and started away from the main actor; the heading, the credit, and the wall's gradient render on the main actor, in a frame after the one in which the chain stands; Core Image compiles the frames' kernels on a stand-in frame, away from the main actor and the render thread; and last the callback goes on. Arming takes about a tenth of a second in the Simulator, and a frame or two of the view still drop shortly after the callback goes on, while RealityKit's rendering takes it up. A push that comes while the recording arms starts it once it is armed, so the video then starts up to that much after the push. The callback and the writer stop when the recording ends.
  - `ImpactFeedback` records the hits from the push on, and the soundtrack is mixed from them once the recording ends (`ImpactMix`): the same low knock that plays live, starting with the first frame that shows each hit, at most one every 30 milliseconds of video unless a hit is much harder, so a run that plays faster than live does not pile up its knocks.
  - Nothing renders again once the recording ends: the encoder takes the last frame again for each frame of the 3-second hold and finishes, the soundtrack is mixed into the movie, and the PNG is written at the same time. **Video** or **Image** chosen before then waits for it, with a spinner in place of **Share**.
  - While a book is out at its details, the recording pauses: the video cuts from the lift to the book's return and leaves out the hits in between. The chain stands still meanwhile, so the run goes on in the video from where it stood. Leaving Desk, including for pushed details, **Back to desk**, sending the app to the background, or a pile image that starts rendering discards the recording before the books have gathered; once they have gathered, the recording ends early and keeps what it has. **Set up again** or another time frame deletes it. Without a recording, **Share** says that a video takes a whole run and offers **Image**: the view as it shows, in the same 9:16 frame, over the spread's area in a view wider than 9:16, with the heading and the credit.
- Pinch to zoom toward a point, and drag to look along the path; either takes the camera over from either mode, from the view as it shows, until the next push or **Set up again**. Both cameras keep moving with the chain while either shows, so switching cameras blends from one moving view to the other, over up to five seconds for a large change of angle. During a setup from the desk, the view keeps showing the books and their places in either mode, and a camera chosen meanwhile takes over once the books stand. A blend, here and after the setup or a book's return, turns onto the new view's subject, such as the falling front, over its first 60 percent while the eye travels over all of it, so the camera never looks past the action on the way. While the chain falls, a blend keeps the front itself in view: the front moves on screen straight from where the old view shows it to where the new one does, over the same 60 percent, so a switch to **Overview** pulls back from the front rather than turning toward the view's middle and sweeping the front out of view (`DominoScene.blended(_:to:progress:keeping:aspect:)`). With **Reduce Motion** on, **Desk options** offers no **Camera**, the camera shows the overview and stays where you leave it, also when a push starts the chain, and no dust drifts in the lamp's beam. Otherwise, fainter dust than the Desk scene's drifts in the beam: the same flecks about 1 to 3 mm long, lit by the lamp, so one that drifts near the low **Follow** camera shows as a speck rather than a large glowing fleck (`DeskStage.setDust(_:fine:)`).
- The lamp's pool of light covers the whole path. Books knock and play haptic taps as they do in the Desk scene, rated for a chain's hits, which change a book's speed by 0.25 to 3.2 units a second rather than a drop's 2 to 24 (`ImpactSound.dominoSpeeds`): in a 39-book chain, half the hits change it by under 0.9 units a second, nine in ten by under 2.1, and the hardest, books landing flat on the table, by about 3, so every strike and landing sounds and taps, louder for a harder one, while the books' settling stays silent, and the throttles keep a long chain to a sound every 30 milliseconds and a tap every 60. A push plays a light tap. Turn **Sounds** off in **Desk options** to silence them. Tilt does not move the dominoes, so **Desk options** offers **Tilt** only for the Pile.
- **Back to desk** hands the books, wherever they lie, back to the Desk scene; switching tabs keeps the chain as you left it (see [Desk](#desk)). The path's framing follows the view's size until the first book drops. While a pile image renders, the dominoes leave their scene, as the live pile does (see [Share the desk](#share-the-desk)).

#### Details from the dominoes

With Reduce Motion off, a book you open lifts out of the chain into its details as a book lifts out of the Desk scene (see [Details from the Desk scene](#details-from-the-desk-scene)), with the same flight, but the dominoes are arranged, so the book is never thrown, and the chain is as it was once the book is back.

- The simulation stands still from the tap until the book is back: falling books hold where they are, mid-fall, and the chain's front, its stall time, and the finale wait. The book rises straight up by its pose alone, colliding with nothing, until its bottom clears the tops of the books around it, its standing neighbors included, then flies to the details over the walnut veil. Books that rest on it, such as the book that fell onto it or a book of the spread that lies across its end, make way so that no book passes through another (`BookExit`): where it can, the book first slides out from under them along its own length or width, as a card is drawn from a fanned deck, about 12 units a second, until it touches no book and nothing lies over it, and they ride down its edge and tip down as far as nothing holds them up, together with any books that rest on them; otherwise, as for a book of the spread with books across both ends, each of them is drawn back along its own length, level, off the book in 0.2 seconds, its near end riding down the book's edge, and tips down beside it before the book rises. Where neither way is clear, such as a book with a rider that has riders of its own and no way to slide, the book still rises straight up through them. Lifting a book tears the cobwebs on either side of it, and the **Follow** camera no longer pushes in on them. While the books set themselves up or drop into their places, and while they gather, a tap does nothing. With Reduce Motion on, the details push instead. When you come back from pushed details, the chain is as you left it.

- The camera gives way to the flight: **Overview** and **Follow** stop moving it, and it rises to look down at the overview's angle, keeping its turn, from the Desk scene's default distance, so the book clears the chain and the veil dims the whole view. When you go back, the camera returns to the camera mode's view as it is then, in the time the book takes to return, so a camera that kept following the chain behind the veil does not travel twice.
- While the details show, the hand, **Set up again**, **Back to desk**, **Share**, **Desk options**, and the time frame button give way to **Back** and **Make share image**, the chain ignores touches, and VoiceOver reads the details and **Back**, and announces nothing about the chain. The escape gesture goes back.
- **Back** fades the details out and returns the book along its flight in reverse: it moves in a straight line to the top of its rise over its place, staying in view and turning to its pose there, then drops straight in, lit like the chain, and comes to rest exactly where it lay, standing in its place in the chain, lying where it fell, or in its place in the spread, in 0.5 seconds in all, with a soft knock (`BookLifter` with `setsDownInPlace`). A book that slid out from under others drops to where its slide ended and slides back in under them, pushing them back up; books drawn back off it tip back up beside it and slide back onto it, in 0.4 seconds more. Every book that made way then lies exactly as it did. It takes back the motion it had, so a book lifted mid-fall falls on. It collides with nothing on the way, knocks no book over, and does not start or push the chain; the simulation then runs on.
- Leaving other than through **Make share image**, a new chain, or a share image that starts rendering closes the details at once and puts the book back where it lay without the flight.

VoiceOver reads the chain as one element with its book count, its time frame, how many cobwebs mark gaps of 30 days or more, and how far it has fallen. It offers **Push the next book** while the chain waits for a push, and **Set up again**, and it announces when the chain stops, when every book has fallen, and, once the books lie gathered, the totals and that **Share** offers a video and an image, or only an image when no recording of the run was kept, except while a book's details show. The setup from the desk, the **Setting up** spinner, the books' drop into their places, the chain's physics and slow motion, the cobwebs and their tearing, the hand, taps and long presses, the details flight and its return and the books that make way for it, both cameras, the worm's rows, the finale's camera move, gathering, and last book, the heading, the video, and Reduce Motion have been checked only in the Simulator, which plays no haptics and whose audio cannot be heard, and neither the cobwebs' fade nor the spinner before books that appear standing has been checked with Reduce Motion on. Check them, a drag that becomes a pinch, a pinch or drag that takes over from **Follow**, switching cameras while the chain runs, the frame rate and the lamp's shadows with 300 books, the frame rate while 300 books set themselves up, whether standing up books with many cobwebs hitches, how the webs' threads and the hand and its glow read on a device's screen, the finale's last landing with its knock and tap, whether recording a run lowers the frame rate, how the recorded video and image look next to the screen, saving and posting them, and the sounds and haptics on a device (see [Check the dominoes' sounds and haptics](#check-the-dominoes-sounds-and-haptics)).

In Debug builds, `-BookwormsTab desk -BookwormsSetUpDominoes YES` chooses **Dominoes** two seconds after the desk's first drop has landed, `-BookwormsSetupLog YES` writes each book's start in a setup from the desk to `Library/Caches/Bookworms/domino-setup.csv`, every overlap of two books during that setup and during the finale's gathering to `domino-overlaps.csv` beside it, and the gathering's books to `domino-gather.csv` (see [Check the dominoes' setup](#check-the-dominoes-setup)), and `-BookwormsDominoPush <seconds>` pushes the next book whenever the chain has stood or stopped that long. `-BookwormsOpenBook <book ID|buried>` lifts that book, or the book with the most books over it, four seconds after every book stands, while the pushes go on, and `-BookwormsPileBack <seconds>` goes back. With `-BookwormsLiftLog YES`, each lift from the dominoes adds a line to `Library/Caches/Bookworms/lift-exit.csv`: the book's ID, how many books rest on it, how far it slides, how far it rises, whether it rises, slides, or draws them back, the milliseconds the plan took, and how far each of those books turns down, in radians, and is drawn back. `-BookwormsDominoCobweb <n>` zooms all the way in on the chain's nth cobweb, counting from 1, once every book stands. `-BookwormsDominoVideo YES` copies each finished recording of a run to `Library/Caches/Bookworms/domino-video-debug.mp4`, with its still at `domino-video-debug.png`. `-BookwormsImpactLog YES` logs every hit of either scene (see [Check the dominoes' sounds and haptics](#check-the-dominoes-sounds-and-haptics)). `-BookwormsDominoCamera <overview|follow>` chooses the camera. It fixes the camera for that launch: **Camera** saves a choice that applies after you relaunch without it, so check switching cameras in a launch without it. `-BookwormsDominoCameraLog YES` logs each frame's time step, front, camera position, direction, and roll, whether the last book has tipped, how upright it stands, and the seconds since the finale started, to `Library/Caches/Bookworms/domino-camera.csv`, and the Follow camera's inputs, with every cobweb's place and density and every book's place and height, to `domino-rig.txt` beside it (see [Check the dominoes' cameras](#check-the-dominoes-cameras)).

### Share

The **Share** tab renders 1080-pixel-wide PNGs in Story (9:16), Portrait (4:5), or Square format and shares them through the share sheet as PNG files named after the template and time frame, such as "Top Five 2025.png". The share sheet can also save them to Photos, after asking once for permission to add to Photos. When the sheet closes, a small toast reports the result for about two seconds, below the Dynamic Island on the iPhone and below the toolbar on the Mac: "Saved to Photos" after **Save Image**, "Copied" after **Copy**, "Shared" after any other app or action, and a short error if one fails. Closing the sheet without sharing shows nothing. VoiceOver announces each toast, and with Reduce Motion it fades instead of sliding. Each template draws one image; Checkout Card draws one of its cards at a time. The tab keeps its mode, template, size, and time frame from one launch to the next, except Desk, Seesaw, and Tall Stack: a launch opens on the template you chose before them, since their images take seconds to render.

On an iPhone, where the tab is taller than wide, the preview stays pinned just below the navigation bar, at up to 380 points and just under half the tab's height (a little less at accessibility text sizes), and the settings scroll beneath it, so each change shows in the image while you make it. The preview lies like a print on a desk blotter: in dark appearance, the table's wood darkened under the pool of the lamp's light, and in light appearance, cream paper, with the settings on paper below it (`ShareBlotter`, `PaperGround`). The print has square corners, a one-pixel cream edge, and the thin shadow of a sheet lying flat, drawn as two offset fills rather than a blur. Increase Contrast leaves out the wood's grain and the lamp's pool. Where the tab is wider than tall, as on the iPhone Duo's inner display in landscape, the preview fills the space beside a 400-point column of settings. The tab's title sits inline in the navigation bar, as Library's and Desk's do, and stays inline while the tab waits for a library. The settings put the template first: choose **My reading** or **With friends**, then a template from the row of tiles under them, each a small drawing of its card, outlined in the accent color while chosen, which scrolls sideways and opens on the chosen template (**My reading** opens on Top Five). Above the default text size, the templates show as a list instead, whose names never truncate. Every tile reserves two lines for its name, so the tiles line up. Below the templates come what chooses the books, then **Image**, then **Options**. Touch and hold the preview to see the image full size at its own aspect on black, over the whole screen, tab bar and navigation bar included; lifting the finger returns to the studio. On a Mac, Quick Look shows it instead (see [On the Mac](#on-the-mac)). A hold starts after a quarter second without movement. Once the tab has drawn an image for an option you changed, such as another template or size, a tip under the preview says to touch and hold it (`HoldPreviewTip`). The launch's first image never shows it, and neither do the images that a sync, a download, or a new spine design draws again without you. Holding the preview, or VoiceOver's **Show full size**, retires the tip, and so does its close button. The tip keeps the first image's options in memory, so drawing an image never writes to TipKit's store. VoiceOver offers the same view as the preview's **Show full size** action; a double-tap or the escape gesture closes it and returns VoiceOver to the preview. The settings are:

- **Compare with**: the reader the **With friends** templates compare with (see [Compare with friends](#compare-with-friends)).
- **Time frame**: **Month**, **Year** (the newest year by default), or **All time**, with a menu of the years or months that have finished books, or for Dropped, dropped books. Templates about several books draw the books finished in the frame, and headings follow it, such as "2025 in books", "My top 5 of August 2025", or "My lifetime in books". A book you reread counts once in each frame you finished it in, and its newest finish there places it in the chart and dates it on Just Finished; a lifetime counts it once, by its newest finish, but its chart still starts at the book's first finish. Just Finished and Review list the frame's books in their book picker; Review's **All time** also lists reviewed books without a finish date. Currently Reading has no time frame.
- **Books**: Top Five's choice of **By rating**, the default, or **Pinned**. **By rating** ranks the books finished in the time frame. **Pinned** draws only your pinned books, of all time, in the order you pinned them, so it replaces **Time frame** with **Pinned books**, and **Show ratings** starts off, separately from its setting for the other images. With no pinned books, the image says so instead of falling back to ratings.
- **Pinned books**: Top Five's favorites, up to five, shown as "2 of 5". The list shows your pinned books first, then every finished book, highest rated first, with a search field; tap a book to pin or unpin it. VoiceOver reads "Pinned" as a pinned book's value. Pins sync through iCloud, so an iPhone and a Mac on the same iCloud account pin the same books; they never reach Hardcover. A pin shows at once from the app's user defaults, then saves as the account's `TopFivePinsV1` record (see [iCloud contract](#icloud-contract)), and each reload shows the pins another device saved. If a save fails, the pin stays on this device and the next pin or reload saves it again. The Apple TV doesn't show Top Five. **Share** hides pins that Top Five can't draw from this device's library, such as a book without a finish date, unless the library has no finished books. The pin stays in the record, so the book returns once this device can draw it and another device keeps it.
- **Book**: the featured book of Just Finished, Review, and Currently Reading. It defaults to the newest choice.
- **Card**: when Checkout Card's time frame fills more than one card, a stepper such as "Card 2 of 3" chooses the card that the preview shows and **Share** sends. Choosing another time frame returns to the first card, and a frame or size with fewer cards shows its last.
- **Image**: the size, and beside it a round glass cube button, **3D books**, whose cube is filled while on; on a Mac, both sit under the preview. With **3D books** on (default), covers draw as 3D books with their spines; the front cover keeps the colors and brightness of the flat cover, and the spine, edges, and boards take the light's shading. Off, covers draw as the whole cover with rounded corners and a soft shadow. Desk and Tall Stack, whose books are always 3D, and Checkout Card, which draws no covers, show no switch. The section's footer says when the switch does less: Year in Books on Square shows no covers, so it disables the switch, and Year in Books draws covers only for its records, which appear when they fit.
- **Options**: **Loved more** or **Loved less** for Hot Takes, **Show ratings**, **Show profile link** on **My reading** images once the app has read your username, and **Shuffle**, which varies Year in Books' spine stacks, the Top Five fan, and a pile's arrangement. The other templates don't offer it.

| Template | Shows |
| --- | --- |
| Year in Books | The frame's totals (books, pages, average rating, and the busiest month, week, or year), a chart of its books as stacks of spines (by month for a year, by week for a month, and by year for a lifetime, leaving out empty years when the books span more than 50), top genres, most-read author, and formats. The longest, shortest, and favorite books appear only where their covers can be at least 200 pixels tall, as they usually can on Story. With them, the chart takes up to 24 percent of the height inside the margins, so their covers can grow. With them, and on Square, the totals sit in one row with shorter labels. Totals, highlights, and records without data are left out. |
| Top Five | Under **By rating**, five of the frame's books: the highest rated, then the most recently finished. Under **Pinned**, your pinned books of all time, in the order you pinned them. Either way, they are fanned like a hand of cards, with a ranked list. Each book out from the middle turns about 7.5 degrees further and sits a little lower on a gentle arc, and the outermost may reach halfway into the side margins. The fan sits midway between the heading and the list. Titles in the list letter at 40 pixels or more and show their subtitles, wrapping to as many as three lines. When the wrapped titles would shrink the fan below 22 percent of a Story's height, 25 percent of a Portrait's, or 28 percent of a Square's, the longest titles shorten one at a time. A shortened title takes one line, dropping its subtitle or series name when a shorter form fits there, or else two lines, keeping its subtitle when the whole title fits in two. |
| Hot Takes | Up to three of the frame's books whose rating is furthest from Hardcover's average, with your stars, the readers' stars, and the difference. **Loved more** shows books you rated at least half a star above the average, and **Loved less** at least half a star below. When only one of them has takes in the frame, or with the chosen reader, the picker switches to it, so the preview isn't blank. Only books with 50 or more Hardcover ratings count. Under **With friends**, it compares with the chosen reader's rating instead, of books you both rated, labels their stars with their first name, and needs no minimum number of Hardcover ratings. Under **With friends**, a lifetime also counts books you rated without a finish date; **My reading** counts only books with a finish date in the frame. One take shows a cover that fills the height under the heading; several share that height as rows. |
| Desk | The frame's finished books spread front cover up on the wooden table under the frame's name and book count, drawn as in [Share the desk](#share-the-desk). Up to 150 books show. The preview shows **Dropping books** while covers load and the books are laid out, which takes a few seconds. **Video** in its options films the same books falling into place (see [Desk video](#desk-video)). |
| Tall Stack | The frame's finished books stacked into one tower on the desk under the lamp, measured at their real thickness, under a headline such as "My 2025 is 1.26 m tall", beside a figure of similar real height, such as a giraffe, with a line that compares them, such as "Taller than a giraffe". See [Tall Stack](#tall-stack). The preview shows **Stacking books** while it renders, which takes a few seconds. **Video** in its options films the tower toppling. |
| Just Finished | One book with your rating and its format, finish date, and length, in a bottom line such as "Finished Nov 30, 2025 · 736 pages", which breaks only between its parts when it is too wide for one line. The title and author, the rating, and that line sit apart as three groups. |
| Review | Your review of one book under its cover, title, and rating. An image with your profile link signs the review at the bottom right, in the link's place, with your name over "@username on Hardcover" beside your round Hardcover photo (or initials), or without a Hardcover name, your @username over "on Hardcover"; one without the link is unsigned. A long review shows as an excerpt that ends with an ellipsis, and the text takes the largest size at which it fits whole; the cover then takes the largest of four heights that leaves the text that size, so a short review gets a large cover and pull-quote type. Only books with a review are offered. Without the iPhone's own Hardcover sign-in, the template uses the reviews in the iCloud library, which a signed-in iPhone or Mac, or a current Apple TV build, saves there. When that library has none, the template says so and offers **Connect Hardcover**. |
| Currently Reading | A book you are reading, with a bar of its progress and its percentage, and a bottom line with its start date when known and its length, such as "Started Oct 2, 2026 · 424 pages". An image with your profile link signs the card in the link's place with your chip, centered and smaller than Review's byline: your round Hardcover photo (or initials) over your name and "@username on Hardcover", or without a Hardcover name, your @username and "on Hardcover". Where height is short, the chip takes one line instead, your photo beside "Name · @username on Hardcover": always on Square, and on Story and Portrait when the stacked chip would leave the book's space shorter than 40 percent of the image's height, as a long title, author list, or details line can. A reread shows its new start date, not its last finish. |
| Taste Match | **With friends** only. Both readers' Hardcover photos (or initials) beside their first names over "Taste match", the match score with a playful name for it (from "Reading twins" at 90 or more down to "Opposite shelves" below 35), how many books you both rated, up to three books you both rated 4 stars or more no more than half a star apart, under "We both loved", and under "We fought over", the books whose ratings are at least 1.5 stars apart, widest first, each with both ratings and a label such as "Friendship on the line" for 3 stars or more: two on Story, one on Portrait and Square. Without such books, it says "Not a single fight. Suspicious." It has no time frame. |
| Checkout Card | The checkout card from a library book's pocket, filled in with the frame's books, oldest first: aged card stock lying on the desk's wood, with a heading filled in as a form is, one line over a red rule: your Hardcover name, else your @username, written before the printed "'s reading record —" and the frame's name written after it, such as "Avery Quill's reading record — 2025", or "Reading record — 2025" until the app has read your Hardcover profile. The heading writes your name whether or not the image letters your profile link, and shows no profile photo. A long name shrinks a little, then gives way to its first word, then ends with an ellipsis. Below the heading come a column of finish dates stamped in violet ink and a column of titles written by hand, each on its own printed rule, as on the date-due card in a book's details. The handwriting is Caveat, downloaded from Google Fonts like the spine fonts, or Noteworthy when it can't download. Each entry varies a little, by its book, in where it starts, its slant, its baseline, and its ink, and about one in four is in a darker pen. A title is written at 82 percent of the rule's spacing and shrinks to fit its line, down to about 40 pixels (85 percent of its size at the closest rules), before it drops its subtitle. It never wraps to another line; a title with no shorter form that still doesn't fit ends with an ellipsis. The rules are 72 pixels apart when the books fit, and closer, down to 58 pixels, when that saves a card. A frame with more books continues on more cards, each filled before the next, so a card holds 17 to 22 books on a Story (16 to 21 with the profile link), 13 to 16 on Portrait, and 9 to 12 on Square; the footer numbers each card, such as "Card 2 of 3", and the file name adds it, such as "Checkout Card 2025 (card 2 of 3).png". |
| Dropped | Black-and-white covers of the books you shelved as **Did Not Finish** in the frame, newest drop first, under "Dropped in 2025" ("Books I dropped" for **All time**) and a count such as "3 books I didn't finish". The count names every dropped book, but the card draws only the newest 26 (`DroppedCard.maximumBooks`), so its spines stay legible; the share text links the books only when there are five or fewer, as for other images. Up to six books show covers; more show spines. Flat, the covers sit in a grid of up to three columns, and spines lie in up to four stacks of lettered spines, newest on top. In 3D, the books stand upright in a wire-mesh wastebasket, covers toward you for up to six books and spines for more, fanned against the rim and leaning a little more for more books; the wires cross in front of them, so they show through. Of five or six books, only the four that the most Hardcover readers rated stand in the basket, so their covers stay large (`DroppedCard.basketCoverLimit`, `[CloudBook].popular(_:)`). The basket runs off the image's bottom edge, with about three quarters of it showing, and fades out above your profile link and the credit, which sit on plain background; on a Story it also reaches into the side margins. A drop date comes from Hardcover (see [iCloud contract](#icloud-contract)); a book without one shows only in **All time**. |
| Book Swap | **With friends** only. Both readers' photos and first names over "Book swap", then picks both ways, under headings with the other reader's first name: under "Robin, read this next", up to three books you rated 4 stars or more that the other reader never shelved, with your stars, and under "My next reads, from Robin", up to three they rated 4 stars or more that you never shelved or have on **Want to Read**, with theirs, highest rated first, then most recently finished. A pick on your Want to Read says "Already on my list" in gold under its title. Books on your other shelves, such as **Paused** or **Did Not Finish**, and books from a library saved without Hardcover shelves (`CloudBook.statusID`), as by an Apple TV build that doesn't download them, never count as picks. A side without picks says so. It has no time frame. On both **With friends** templates, the cover rows grow by up to 40 percent into height the card has to spare. |
| Seesaw | **With friends** only. Your books finished in the time frame and the other reader's, drawn as 3D books like Desk's, stacked on the ends of an oak plank that rests on a walnut fulcrum on the Desk's table under the lamp: yours on the left, theirs on the right. Each side weighs its pages, counting a book without a page count as 320 pages, as the 3D books' sizes and masses do (`SeesawBalance`). Like a playground seesaw, the plank tips all the way to the side with more pages, however small the difference, until that end rests on the table; equal pages leave it level. Each end shows up to 28 books in two stacks, the most popular first by Hardcover ratings count, then the newest, with the biggest books at the bottom of each stack. Over the seesaw are the frame's name and who read more, such as "Robin read 1,203 more pages" ("Perfectly balanced" for a tie); under it, each reader's photo, first name, and counts for the whole frame, such as "39 books · 19,323 pages", under their end. The room behind the table takes the image's color family. The preview shows **Weighing books** while covers load. Covers that miss the 10-second wait show the book's title on the cloth until they arrive, and the image then draws again, as Desk's does. `SeesawScene` builds the scene and `SeesawRenderer` renders it offscreen, as `PileSnapshotRenderer` renders Desk, taking turns with it. Seesaw has no **Shuffle**. |

On a Story, every template keeps its content out of the 250-pixel strips at the top and bottom that the platforms' bars cover, except that Top Five, Hot Takes from **My reading**, Just Finished, Currently Reading, and Checkout Card may reach up to 100 pixels into the bottom strip: Top Five's last ranked row, Hot Takes' last take or ratings, the details line under Just Finished's and Currently Reading's book, and the foot of Checkout Card's card. The bottom 150 pixels stay free for the platforms' reply bar and the credit. Just Finished, Currently Reading, and Review sit 48 pixels higher on a Story and 24 on Portrait, so their book and title sit a little above the middle, and on a Story the book may reach that far into the top strip. On an image with your profile link, these templates reach only 20 pixels into the strip, and the link takes the 80 pixels above the bottom 150. Currently Reading's chip and Review's byline take the link's place, with their bottom edge where the link's is, 8 pixels above the bottom 150. These templates other than Checkout Card spend the extra height only on wider gaps, so their covers and text keep the sizes that the 250-pixel margin gives them; Checkout Card's card grows by that height, which can give it room for another line. Dropped's wastebasket runs off the bottom edge in 3D, fading into the background behind the credit. Top Five and Hot Takes share it evenly among their gaps; Just Finished and Currently Reading give a quarter to each gap within the details and the rest to the gap under the book.

Each image's backgrounds and panels come from one color family: the hue its books' spines share most, or a warm neutral when they are mostly gray. Unrelated spine colors never compete in the background; covers and spines keep their own colors. Text is cream with gold accents. Checkout Card is the exception: its card keeps the card stock and inks of the app's paper props (`Stationery`). Text on every other image is set in New York, in each book's own capitalization, with sentence-case headings in the text's cream, such as "We both loved". The spine of a 3D book keeps its designed font and colors. A 3D book without a downloaded cover shows its title on the front in the spine's font.

Lettered titles leave out a trailing series tag, such as "(Dungeon Crawler Carl, #4)" or "(The Expanse 3)": a parenthetical that holds a "#", ends in a number, or starts with the series name. Each title takes the largest size that fits its space, down to the template's smallest size. When the full title doesn't fit even then, the image drops its subtitle, the text after the first ": " or " — ", so *Capital: A Critique of Political Economy, Volume 1* becomes *Capital*. When the text before the colon is the series name, the image drops that prefix instead and keeps the rest, so "Star Wars: Thrawn" in the series "Star Wars" becomes "Thrawn". A title that has no shorter form and still doesn't fit ends with an ellipsis. Templates about one book usually have room for the full title. The spine and coverless front of a 3D book letter the title before its first colon (`CloudBook.spineTitle`), as the Shelf's spines do.

Images from **My reading** show only your own library. Once this device's Hardcover sign-in has read your profile, which happens with the friends' data (see [Compare with friends](#compare-with-friends)), they letter your profile link, such as "hardcover.app/@username", centered in the bottom margin, and the preview shows it (Review signs the review with your photo, name, and username instead, and Currently Reading with your centered chip, each reaching down to where the link sits): on a Story just above the bottom 150 pixels, so the platforms' reply bar doesn't cover it, and on Portrait and Square just above the credit. A long username shrinks the link to fit one line. Turn off **Show profile link** in **Options**, on by default, to leave the link out of the image and of the share text; **Share desk** offers the same switch, and the two share one setting. Until the app has read your profile, and without a sign-in, the images carry no account name or ID. Images from **With friends** show both readers' first names and photos, their ratings (both readers' on Hot Takes and Taste Match, and the recommending reader's on each Book Swap pick), and your Hardcover username in the invitation at the bottom (see [Compare with friends](#compare-with-friends)). Ratings are optional on the templates that offer **Show ratings**. Every shared image carries a small "Created with Bookworms for iPhone" credit a few pixels above its bottom edge, in the bottom margin that every template keeps free, which on a Story is the bottom 150 pixels. The preview leaves the credit out; sharing adds it (`UIImage.withShareCredit()`). On a Story image, Instagram's reply bar can cover it on phones where the bar overlaps the story. If an image cannot be drawn, such as a pile whose frame comes out one flat color, the preview says so, suggesting **Shuffle** for a pile, and nothing is shared.

A new option draws a new image and cancels the one in progress. A new design for a book in the image, such as one a generation run saves every few seconds, draws after the image in progress instead, and the shown image stays shareable meanwhile. Designs for other books change nothing.

#### View in my room

On an iPhone that tracks the world (`ARWorldTrackingConfiguration.isSupported`), the share sheet that **Share** opens lists **View in my room** below its system actions (`RoomCamera.action`, a `ShareAction`). It shows for the templates of your own books that a time frame chooses, except Dropped, whose books you didn't read, and only when the frame has books. Choosing it closes the sheet and opens the camera full screen (`RoomPhotoView`). The Mac and the Apple TV have no AR camera, so their share pickers never list it, and the app's own screens gain nothing.

- **Books.** Every book finished in the image's time frame, up to the 40 most popular by Hardcover ratings count (`RoomPlacement.maximumBooks`, `[CloudBook].popular(_:)`), in finishing order. Forty typical books stack about 1.2 m tall, and their textures leave the camera room on older iPhones. The books wait for their fonts and up to 10 seconds for their covers, as the Desk's do (`BookFactory`).
- **Stack.** One stack, built as the tall-stack share template builds it (`BookTower`), oldest at the bottom, stands at life size where the screen's center meets a level surface, its spines toward the camera, and follows that point along the surface until you tap. A tap sets it down, and another picks it up again. Scene units are decimeters and ARKit's are meters, so each placement's root scales by 0.1 (`RoomPlacement.root(at:facing:)`). A placement is only as true as ARKit's scale: close on iPhones with LiDAR, a few percent off on others.
- **Shelf.** On iPhones with LiDAR (`RoomCamera.supportsShelf`), a **Stack** / **Shelf** switch sits above the shutter; other iPhones get the stack alone, since without the scene mesh books could stand through the shelf above or over a panel in front of them. In **Shelf**, rays through the scene mesh measure the empty space around the point on the board (`ShelfProbe`): the back within 60 cm behind it, the clearance up to the shelf above, and each end, where a ray along the board meets a side panel or another object or the board itself ends. The books stand in it spines out from the left end, fore edges near the back, the last three lying flat as a bookend (`ShelfFill`). The shelf shows the most popular books that fit, in finishing order; books taller than the clearance are left out, three books or fewer lie flat as one small stack, and a long shelf leaves its right end empty. The probes run every sixth frame, and the row lays out again only when the space changes by more than a centimeter.
- **Look.** The room lights the books: ARKit's environment texturing lights their jackets, they cast grounding shadows on the surface and on each other, people in front of them hide them (`personSegmentationWithDepth`), and on iPhones with LiDAR, so does furniture in front of them. No physics runs.
- **Photo.** The system coaching overlay shows until ARKit finds a level surface. The camera dims outside the part the share image keeps, at the chosen size (`RoomPhoto.photoRect`). The shutter takes `ARView.snapshot`, which holds the camera image and the books but not the reticle or the controls, and opens the share sheet with the photo over a band in the books' colors (`ShareTheme`): "My 2026 stack" or "My 2026 shelf", and the count with the stack's height or the shelf's length in the iPhone's units (`BookTower.heightText`), such as "11 books, 32 cm tall" or "14 books, 54 cm of shelf". The band's text stays above a Story's bottom strip. The share text links the books, as **My reading** images' does.
- **Privacy.** The first use asks for the camera (`NSCameraUsageDescription`). Camera frames and the scene mesh stay in ARKit's memory; the app stores and uploads neither, and the photo exists only until the share sheet writes its file. Closing the view pauses the session and releases the books' textures. Each 3D view takes its books out while a share image renders, and this one does too (`PileSnapshotRenderer.isRendering`).

The Simulator has no AR camera, so the unit tests cover the placement, the shelf's measurement and fill, the units, and the framing (`RoomPlacementTests`); the camera itself needs an iPhone.

### Compare with friends

**With friends** compares your ratings with another Hardcover reader's. It needs this device's Hardcover sign-in; without it, the tab offers **Connect Hardcover**. The **Compare with** row shows the chosen reader's photo, name, username, and match score, and **View @username on Hardcover** opens their profile, in the Hardcover app when it is installed. The tab first shows the best match. Tapping the row (on a Mac, clicking it, which opens a popover) lists everyone you can compare with, best match first, in **Following** and **Looked up**, each with a button that opens their Hardcover profile, also in the row's context menu. The search field filters the list by name or username. A username that no listed reader has, typed with or without "@" or pasted as a `hardcover.app/@username` link, offers **Compare with @username**, which looks the reader up on Hardcover. Hardcover ignores capitals in the username, so `samreads` finds `SamReads`. A lookup says so when the username is invalid, no public reader has it (a private profile also reads as not found), it is your own, you blocked the reader, or Hardcover shows none of their ratings. The list keeps the five readers you looked up most recently. Pull down in the list to refresh it, or on a Mac, click **Refresh** below it.

**Match score:** 100 minus 50 points for each star of the average gap between your rating and theirs, over every book you both rated, from 0 to 100. Identical ratings score 100, an average gap of one star scores 50, and two stars or more score 0. Ratings outside 0.5 to 5 stars count as unrated. With fewer than five books you both rated, there is no score, and Taste Match says how many you have. `TasteMatch` in `Bookworms/Models/SocialLibrary.swift` holds the formula and the thresholds.

**Invitation:** every **With friends** image ends with "Compare takes with me on Hardcover · hardcover.app/@username", with your Hardcover username, on two lines when one doesn't fit, and **Share** sends the text "Compare takes with me on Hardcover: https://hardcover.app/@username" with the image, which apps that take text, such as Messages, include. **My reading** and **Share desk** images send a line with each book's title and permanent Hardcover page, such as "Dune: https://hardcover.app/id/books/123", when they draw five books or fewer (for Checkout Card, the books on the card shared), then "My reading on Hardcover: https://hardcover.app/@username" once the app has read your profile; the images letter the same profile link. Apps that drop text attached to an image, as Instagram and TikTok may, keep only the lettered link. On the Mac, the share menu lists the services that take the image, including **Add to Photos**, which takes no text and receives the image alone; the others receive the text too. `HardcoverInvite` in `Bookworms/Models/SocialLibrary.swift` holds the wording and the links.

**Data:** after each library reload while signed in, when the friends' data is missing or a day old (or at once when you pull to refresh), the app reads your Hardcover profile, the readers you blocked, and the readers you follow, leaving out blocked readers. It then downloads, one reader at a time, the libraries of up to 15 followed readers, those with events among Hardcover's 100 newest for your followed readers first, then in the order you followed them, and of every reader you looked up, refreshing only libraries a day old or older. A followed reader outside that limit downloads when shown. Each library holds the reader's rated books, except those they marked Ignored, and the books on their Currently Reading, Read, Paused, and Did Not Finish shelves, highest rated first, up to 3,000 books, with their ratings, finish dates, and covers chosen as for your own books, but no reviews. Lists download 100 rows per request, about a second apart. Comparisons are prepared off the main thread whenever either library changes. Seesaw weighs the reader's finished books from this library, so for a reader past 3,000 books it can miss lower-rated reads.

A failed refresh keeps the saved data, reports the failure under **Compare with**, and waits five minutes before an automatic retry. A reader whose ratings fail to download when shown is reported there too, until a download or a refresh succeeds; choosing the reader again retries. A rejected login or denied social access deletes the friends' data. Each refresh of the people you follow removes unfollowed and blocked readers and their ratings. A reader whose library Hardcover denies, or returns empty, as for a private profile, shows "No public ratings". Book Swap counts a book as shelved by the other reader when it is anywhere in their downloaded library; your own library includes every shelf.

## Look and feel

The app follows one direction, room and paper: system Liquid Glass for bars, the sidebar, sheets, and menus, tinted only with the accent color, and the app's character in the content beneath it. Library, Desk, and book details are a walnut room, dark in either appearance; the screens outside the room, such as Share and Spines, can sit on paper. The shared pieces live in `Bookworms/Views/RoomAndPaper.swift` and the asset catalog. Build new screens from them rather than from new colors, grounds, or type styles. They are gradients and static images, which cost nothing while content scrolls or animates: add no shaders, blur layers, or timers to them.

- **Accent color.** `AccentColor` is dark brass, `#8A4F17`, in light appearance and a lighter brass, `#CC8540`, in dark. Under Increase Contrast, light appearance uses a darker brass, `#6E3B0E`, for 7:1 text, and dark appearance `#CB843F`. `project.yml` makes it the app's global accent. In dark appearance, a brass rather than the lamp's pale gold keeps the white symbols and labels that filled buttons and toggles draw at 3:1 contrast or more, as the system's dark blue does, and accent text at about 5.6:1 on dark rows. A lighter brass would raise that text contrast but drop the white labels below 3:1, so the dark High Contrast variant keeps nearly the same color, and accent-filled controls keep white labels in every appearance. Keep that variant at least one step away from the standard dark color: when the two are identical, the system brightens the accent itself under Increase Contrast, to about `#FFA54D` in the iOS 27 Simulator, which leaves white labels at 2:1. Library and Desk tint their own controls with the lamp's color (`ShelfPalette.lamp`). On the Mac, the system uses the app's accent only when **Accent color** in System Settings is **Multicolor**.
- **The room.** `ShelfPalette` names the walnut room's colors, which live in the asset catalog (`ShelfWallTop`, `ShelfWallBottom`, `ShelfBoard`, `ShelfLamp`, `ShelfPaper`, and `ShelfGold`). Each color that text or controls use or sit on has a High Contrast variant. `WalnutWall` draws the wall: the gradient, the grain (`WallGrain`) at 12%, and the seams, which Increase Contrast leaves out. Desk draws the plain gradient (`ShelfPalette.wall`), because its scene's table covers the wall, and the grain and seams would only blend under every RealityKit frame. Fill the screen with it, ignoring the safe area, so walls that fade over each other keep their panels lined up; pass `opacity` to fade it rather than fading the view.
- **Paper.** `PaperGround` is the icon's cream, `#FCF3E0` to `#EFE0C2`, in light appearance and ink walnut, `#1B140F` to `#0F0B08`, in dark (`PaperTop` and `PaperBottom`), under a faint dither (`PaperDither`). Put it behind a screen's content, such as a `Form` with `.scrollContentBackground(.hidden)`; never use it as a window, sheet, or presentation background. The Spines tab sits on it, and so do the Share tab's settings on the iPhone and its preview in light appearance (`ShareBlotter`).
